\chapter{Sample Designs in R}\label{sample-designs-in-r}

The method of anlalysis of survey data depends crucially on its sample
design. Simple methods of analysis which neglect the sample design can
lead to estimates which are severely biased, and standard errors that
are incorrect.

In the this and the following Chapter we'll use the \texttt{survey} package in R to carry out analyses.
We'll use the \texttt{schools} data set as our primary example, but also use
data on populations and Gross Domestic Product for each country in 2023
from the `Our World in Data' website.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Census}
\NormalTok{schools.census }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ schools }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{select.srswor}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(schools))}
\CommentTok{\# SRSWOR}
\NormalTok{schools.srswor }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ schools }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{select.srswor}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{)}
\CommentTok{\# PPSWR}
\NormalTok{schools.ppswr }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ schools }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{select.ppswr}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{sizevar=}\StringTok{"Total"}\NormalTok{)}
\CommentTok{\# STSRS}
\NormalTok{schools.stsrs }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ schools }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{select.stsrs}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{60}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{stratumvar=}\StringTok{"Education\_Region"}\NormalTok{)}
\CommentTok{\# 1SC}
\NormalTok{schools}\FloatTok{.1}\NormalTok{sc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ schools }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{select.1sc}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{clustervar=}\StringTok{"Territorial\_Authority"}\NormalTok{)}
\CommentTok{\# 2SC}
\NormalTok{schools}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{sc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ schools }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{select.2sc}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cfraction=}\FloatTok{0.20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mmin=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }
                                     \AttributeTok{clustervar=}\StringTok{"Territorial\_Authority"}\NormalTok{)}
\CommentTok{\# ST2SC}
\NormalTok{schools.st2sc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ schools }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{select.st2sc}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{stratumvar=}\StringTok{"Region"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{allocation=}\StringTok{"proportional"}\NormalTok{,}
                                \AttributeTok{cfraction=}\FloatTok{0.20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{clustervar=}\StringTok{"Territorial\_Authority"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

In order to make estimates from sampled data using the \texttt{survey} package
we need to specify the sample design we're using.
We'll do this for the various sample designs covered in the
previous chapter.

\begin{quote}
Note that in the \texttt{survey} package we identify variables with their names without
quotes and with a \texttt{\textasciitilde{}} symbol in front. (If you're interested you can
use the \texttt{survyr} package which is a \texttt{dplyr}-like wrapper for \texttt{survey}
with functions defined so that the \texttt{\textasciitilde{}} isn't necessary.)
\end{quote}

We'll define each design, and then use it to estimate the mean
size of school rolls in the population (stored in the variable \texttt{Total} in
the \texttt{schools} data frame. (One exception will be the PPSWR example
where we'll select a sample of countries using their population size
as the size variable.)

The true mean school size is

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ybar }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(schools}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Total)}
\NormalTok{ybar}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## [1] 334.481
\end{verbatim}

\section{Census}\label{census-1}

The analysis of a census is trivial (at least, if there is no non-response).
We can create a census dataset by taking an SRSWOR of size \(n=N\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{schools.census }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ schools }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{select.srswor}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(schools))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This function adds the column \texttt{bigN} to the dataset, which contains
population size \(N\) repeated on every row.

Now we can specify the design.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{schools.census.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{id=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{schools.census, }\AttributeTok{fpc=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{bigN)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Note that we tell R which column contains the population size using the \texttt{fpc} argument.
Our data aren't clustered, so the \texttt{id} variable (which identifies clusters) is set to 1.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Total, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{schools.census.des, }\AttributeTok{deff=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##         mean     SE DEff
## Total 334.48   0.00  NaN
\end{verbatim}

The estimated mean is 334.48, which is of course
exactly equal to the population mean - and note that the standard error is zero:
\textbf{censuses have no sampling error}. We've measured everything there is to measure
so we have no uncertainty.

We've requested \texttt{Deff=TRUE}, and have got a Deff value of NaN, which means
the Deff was not able to be calculated. We'll discuss what this means later on.

If we had left out the \texttt{fpc} command

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{schools.census.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{id=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{schools.census)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## Warning in svydesign.default(id = ~1, data = schools.census): No weights or
## probabilities supplied, assuming equal probability
\end{verbatim}

we get a warning that we can interpret as R assuming an SRSWR from an infinite population.

The estimated mean is the same, but the standard error are now what'd we'd have learned
from first year statistics.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Total, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{schools.census.des, }\AttributeTok{deff=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##           mean       SE DEff
## Total 334.4810   8.4582  Inf
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{sy }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(schools.census}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Total)}
\NormalTok{n }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(schools.census)}
\NormalTok{se }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ sy}\SpecialCharTok{/}\FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(n)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

i.e.~the standard error is
\[
   \bfa{SE}{\widehat{\bar{Y}}} 
   = \frac{s_y}{\sqrt{n}} 
   = \frac{423.162}{\sqrt{2503}} = 8.458
\]

Also notice that the \texttt{Deff} value has changed to \texttt{Inf} (meaning infinity) -
again we'll come back to what this means this shortly.

\section{Simple Random Sample WithOut Replacement (SRSWOR)}\label{simple-random-sample-without-replacement-srswor}

Let's draw a sample of \(n=60\) schools from the population by SRSWOR.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{schools.srswor }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ schools }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{select.srswor}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{60}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Here are the top 5 rows of the 60 in the sample

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{r|l|l|l|r|r}
\hline
School\_Id & Org\_Name & Add2\_City & Regional\_Council & bigN & weight\\
\hline
3193 & Hampden Street School & Nelson & Nelson Region & 2503 & 41.71667\\
\hline
1519 & Sunnyvale School & Waitakere & Auckland Region & 2503 & 41.71667\\
\hline
202 & Palmerston North Boys' High School & Palmerston North & Manawatū-Whanganui Region & 2503 & 41.71667\\
\hline
1234 & Bombay School & Bombay & Auckland Region & 2503 & 41.71667\\
\hline
3396 & Kahikatea Kirkwood Intermediate & Christchurch & Canterbury Region & 2503 & 41.71667\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

To specify the design we use the same syntax as above:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{schools.srswor.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{id=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{schools.srswor, }\AttributeTok{fpc=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{bigN)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Estimating the mean school size is also just the same:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Total, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{schools.srswor.des, }\AttributeTok{deff=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean      SE DEff
## Total 347.367  49.582    1
\end{verbatim}

This is the mean of a genuine sample now, so we have an estimated mean value that
differs from the truth, and the standard error is the gauge of its accuracy.

The standard error comes from the finite population corrected formula:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{bigN }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{first}\NormalTok{(schools.srswor}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{bigN) }\CommentTok{\# take the entry in the first row: they\textquotesingle{}re all the same}
\NormalTok{sy }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(schools.srswor}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Total)}
\NormalTok{n }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(schools.srswor)}
\NormalTok{se }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{n}\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{bigN)}\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{sy}\SpecialCharTok{/}\FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(n)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\[
   \bfa{SE}{\widehat{\bar{Y}}} 
   = \sqrt{1-\frac{n}{N}}\frac{s_y}{\sqrt{n}} 
   = \sqrt{1-\frac{60}{2503}}
     \frac{388.745}{\sqrt{60}} = 49.582
\]

Compare this to the simple SE, which is just slightly bigger

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{se }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ sy}\SpecialCharTok{/}\FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(n)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\[
   \bfa{SE}{\widehat{\bar{Y}}} 
   = \frac{s_y}{\sqrt{n}} 
   = \frac{388.745}{\sqrt{60}} = 50.187
\]
This is what we get if we omit the \texttt{fpc}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{schools.srswor.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{id=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{schools.srswor)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## Warning in svydesign.default(id = ~1, data = schools.srswor): No weights or
## probabilities supplied, assuming equal probability
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Total, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{schools.srswor.des, }\AttributeTok{deff=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean      SE DEff
## Total 347.367  50.187  Inf
\end{verbatim}

\section{Probability Proportional to Size With Replacement (PPSWR)}\label{probability-proportional-to-size-with-replacement-ppswr}

We have a dataset from `Our World in Data' giving population sizes,
Gross Domestic Product and military expenditure in 2023 (all in US dollars).
The first 10 rows are below:

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|l|r|r|l|r|r|r}
\hline
Country & Code & GovtSpend & GDP & Region & Population & GDPPerCapita & MilSpend\\
\hline
Albania & ALB & 1863822443 & 1.496298e+10 & Europe & 2811660 & 5321.759 & 397617600\\
\hline
Algeria & DZA & 44321604142 & 2.151440e+11 & Africa & 46164222 & 4660.405 & 18263968000\\
\hline
Angola & AGO & 15741572839 & 1.032363e+11 & Africa & 36749909 & 2809.157 & 1247846300\\
\hline
Argentina & ARG & 111098899281 & 5.916847e+11 & South America & 45538402 & 12993.093 & 4882353000\\
\hline
Armenia & ARM & 1598415044 & 1.540616e+10 & Asia & 2943398 & 5234.142 & 1326245800\\
\hline
Australia & AUS & 385802798739 & 1.649425e+12 & Oceania & 26451126 & 62357.456 & 32387990000\\
\hline
Austria & AUT & 84758230499 & 4.246078e+11 & Europe & 9130434 & 46504.669 & 4452228000\\
\hline
Azerbaijan & AZE & NA & 5.755883e+10 & Asia & 10318209 & 5578.374 & 3561670700\\
\hline
Bahrain & BHR & 6908975351 & 3.996681e+10 & Asia & 1569674 & 25461.855 & 1622393700\\
\hline
Bangladesh & BGD & 18377804014 & 3.232800e+11 & Asia & 171466986 & 1885.377 & 3782793000\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

We can select a PPSWR using population as the size variable

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{mil.ppswr }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ mil }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{select.ppswr}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{sizevar=}\StringTok{"Population"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The \texttt{weight} column here combines the weight over the number of times a unit is selected

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{mil.ppswr.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{id=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{mil.ppswr, }\AttributeTok{weight=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{weight)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The mean military expenditure per country is

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{MilSpend, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{mil.ppswr.des, }\AttributeTok{deff=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##                mean         SE   DEff
## MilSpend 7.4657e+10 3.4650e+10 1.0362
\end{verbatim}

though more interesting is the \textbf{total} of this variable, to see
how much the world is spending.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svytotal}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{MilSpend, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{mil.ppswr.des, }\AttributeTok{deff=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##               total         SE  DEff
## MilSpend 1.1199e+13 5.3452e+12 1.096
\end{verbatim}

\begin{quote}
The estimation of the \textbf{total} of a variable is only something
that makes sense in a finite population, and is a type of estimate
that is rarely found anywhere outside sample survey analysis.

It is frequently of interest in Official (i.e.~government) statistics
that we want to estimate the total number of people with a certain characteristic,
or the total amount of a commodity being exported, or total export earnings.
\end{quote}

Note again that in a with replacement design we can't apply the finite
population correction.

\section{Stratified Sample}\label{stratified-sample}

Recall that in this design we specify a set of groups called strata, and
each population unit belongs to one of these strata.

Selecting a sample of \(n=60\) schools by equal allocation across the 12
Education Regions:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{schools.stsrs }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ schools }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{select.stsrs}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{60}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{stratumvar=}\StringTok{"Education\_Region"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Specify this design

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{schools.stsrs.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{id=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{strata=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Education\_Region, }\AttributeTok{fpc=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Nh,}
                               \AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{schools.stsrs,}
                               \AttributeTok{survey.lonely.psu=}\StringTok{"remove"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Note that we've included the option \texttt{survey.lonely.psu="remove"} to mean
that the estimation of standard errors doesn't fail if a stratum only has a single
PSU.

We can now return to estimating the mean school size

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Total, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{schools.stsrs.des, }\AttributeTok{deff=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean      SE   DEff
## Total 337.235  39.972 0.6816
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Design Effect}\label{design-effect}

Now it's finally time to talk about the \textbf{Design Effect}, or the Deff for short.

The Deff of an estimator \(\widehat{T}\) of a population characteristic \(T\) in
a particular sample design is defined to be
\[
   \bfa{Deff}{\widehat{T}_{\text{design}}}
   = 
   \frac{\bfa{Var}{\widehat{T}_{\text{design}}}}{
         \bfa{Var}{\widehat{T}_{\text{SRSWOR}}}}
   = 
   \left(\frac{\bfa{SE}{\widehat{T}_{\text{design}}}}{
               \bfa{SE}{\widehat{T}_{\text{SRSWOR}}}}\right)^2
\]
where both estimators are based on samples of the same size.

It tells us how the standard error differs between our design of
interest and a SRSWOR of the same sample size.

Note that

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  We have a different Deff for every estimate we make from a survey.
\item
  A Deff\textless1 means that the Standard Errors are smaller than what
  we'd get in SRSWOR. A small Deff indicates a \textbf{more efficient} design.
\item
  A Deff of 1 means the design is no better than SRSWOR - and of course
  (look back above) the Deffs of all estimates in SRSWOR are 1 by definition.
\item
  If we're dealing with a \textbf{census}, then the Standard Error is zero,
  and in any case there's no difference between designs, so the Deff
  isn't defined. (That's why it was \texttt{NaN} or \texttt{Inf} in our census examples.)
\item
  \textbf{Stratified designs} can achieve Deffs much much less than 1 if their strata
  are well defined to be informative about the estimates of interest. e.g.
  in business surveys where the strata are defined by business size we
  can get very good Deffs.
\item
  \textbf{Cluster designs} often have Deffs greater than 1, which show them to be
  significantly less efficient than SRSWOR.
\end{itemize}

The Deff values we get from R are approximations, and aren't very reliable
when sample sizes are small. But do notice that we have a Deff\textless1 here
for the stratified sample of \(n=60\) units, and the SE is lower than
that achieved in the SRSWOR of \(n=60\) units above.

\section{One stage cluster design (1SC)}\label{one-stage-cluster-design-1sc}

A one stage cluster design takes \(n=10\) Territorial Authority areas (TAs) at random
and selects all schools in each selected TA.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{schools}\FloatTok{.1}\NormalTok{sc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ schools }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{select.1sc}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{clustervar=}\StringTok{"Territorial\_Authority"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

We need a column \texttt{bigN} which gives the number of TAs in the population.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{schools}\FloatTok{.1}\NormalTok{sc.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{id=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Territorial\_Authority, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{schools}\FloatTok{.1}\NormalTok{sc, }
                             \AttributeTok{fpc=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{bigN)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Total, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{schools}\FloatTok{.1}\NormalTok{sc.des, }\AttributeTok{deff=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean      SE   DEff
## Total 287.197  43.829 5.7634
\end{verbatim}

The Deff of this one stage cluster sample is bad (much larger than 1) - this is due to
strong differences between TAs in the mean school size. The more the clusters differ from
each other on the characteristic of interest, the worse the Deff gets.

\section{Two stage cluster design (2SC)}\label{two-stage-cluster-design-2sc}

A two stage cluster design takes \(n=10\) Territorial Authority areas (TAs) at random
and selects 20\% of the schools in each selected TA.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{schools}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{sc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ schools }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{select.2sc}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cfraction=}\FloatTok{0.20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mmin=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }
                                     \AttributeTok{clustervar=}\StringTok{"Territorial\_Authority"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

We need a column \texttt{bigN1} which gives the number of TAs in the population, and
\texttt{bigN2} which is the number of schools in each TA. We also need to specify names
of unique identifiers of the first stage units (the PSUs: these are Territorial Authorities)
and the second stage units (the SSUs: these are Schools).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{schools}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{sc.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{id=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Territorial\_Authority}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{School\_Id, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{schools}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{sc, }
                             \AttributeTok{fpc=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{bigN1}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{bigN2,}
                             \AttributeTok{survey.lonely.psu=}\StringTok{"remove"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Total, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{schools}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{sc.des, }\AttributeTok{deff=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean      SE   DEff
## Total 486.981  84.261 2.1739
\end{verbatim}

This design specification has the fpc specified at both stages of selection,
and assumes SRSWOR at both stages. The \texttt{survey} package currently only allows for the analysis
of two stage cluster designs with PPSWR at the first stage of selection. We'll stick
to considering designs with SRSWOR at both stages.

For the record, this is how to select a two stage cluster sample with PPSWR at the
first stage, and how to specify its sample design in R:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{schools}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{sc.ppswr }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ schools }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{select.2sc}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }
                                           \AttributeTok{method1=}\StringTok{"PPSWR"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{sizevar1=}\StringTok{"Total"}\NormalTok{,}
                                           \AttributeTok{cfraction=}\FloatTok{0.20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mmin=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }
                                     \AttributeTok{clustervar=}\StringTok{"Territorial\_Authority"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{schools}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{sc.ppswr.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{id=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Territorial\_Authority}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{School\_Id, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{schools}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{sc.ppswr, }
                                   \AttributeTok{weight=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{weight,}
                                   \AttributeTok{survey.lonely.psu=}\StringTok{"remove"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Total, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{schools}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{sc.ppswr.des, }\AttributeTok{deff=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean      SE   DEff
## Total 437.854  80.806 1.6635
\end{verbatim}

\section{Stratified two stage cluster design}\label{stratified-two-stage-cluster-design}

Finally here is a stratified two stage sample of schools within TA's with Regions.
The Regions are strata, the TAs are clusters within the Regions, and then 20\% of
schools are sampled in the selected TAs.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{schools.st2sc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ schools }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{select.st2sc}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{stratumvar=}\StringTok{"Region"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{allocation=}\StringTok{"proportional"}\NormalTok{,}
                                         \AttributeTok{cfraction=}\FloatTok{0.20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{clustervar=}\StringTok{"Territorial\_Authority"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The design is the same as the two stage cluster sample, except we add the stratum specification.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{schools.st2sc.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{id=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Territorial\_Authority}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{School\_Id, }\AttributeTok{strata=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Region, }
                               \AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{schools.st2sc, }
                               \AttributeTok{fpc=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{bigN1}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{bigN2,}
                               \AttributeTok{survey.lonely.psu=}\StringTok{"remove"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Total, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{schools.st2sc.des, }\AttributeTok{deff=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean      SE  DEff
## Total 295.326  25.898 1.248
\end{verbatim}

\chapter{Survey Analysis}\label{survey-analysis}

With the ability to load a survey dataset and specify its survey design
we're now ready to do some analysis.

We'll use the \texttt{api} dataset which comes with the survey package: it's a
set of observations of the Academic Performance Index (API) on all
California Schools with at least 100 students in the years 1999 and 2000.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{data}\NormalTok{(api)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

In particular we'll use the \texttt{apiclus2} dataset, which is a two stage
cluster sample of 126 schools drawn from the population of 6194.
The top 10 rows of the data set and some of the key variables are shown below.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{apiclus2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{stype }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{factor}\NormalTok{(apiclus2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{stype, }\AttributeTok{levels=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"E"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"M"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"H"}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{levels}\NormalTok{(apiclus2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{stype) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Elementary"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"Middle"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"High"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{apiclus2 }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{slice\_head}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(cds, stype, name, dnum, cnum, api99, api00) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{kbl}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{kable\_styling}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|l|l|r|r|r|r}
\hline
cds & stype & name & dnum & cnum & api99 & api00\\
\hline
31667796031017 & Elementary & Alta-Dutch Flat & 15 & 30 & 785 & 821\\
\hline
55751846054837 & Elementary & Tenaya Elementa & 63 & 54 & 718 & 773\\
\hline
41688746043517 & Elementary & Panorama Elemen & 83 & 40 & 632 & 600\\
\hline
41688746043509 & Middle & Lipman Middle & 83 & 40 & 740 & 740\\
\hline
41688746043491 & Elementary & Brisbane Elemen & 83 & 40 & 711 & 716\\
\hline
40687266042998 & Elementary & Cayucos Element & 117 & 39 & 779 & 811\\
\hline
20651936023881 & Elementary & Fuller (Merle L & 132 & 19 & 432 & 472\\
\hline
20651936023931 & Elementary & Fairmead Elemen & 132 & 19 & 494 & 520\\
\hline
20651936023907 & Middle & Wilson Elementa & 132 & 19 & 589 & 568\\

06615980631259 & High & Colusa High & 152 & 5 & 585 & 591\\

\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The full set of variables in the dataset is:

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|l}
\hline
Variable & Description\\
\hline
cds & Unique identifier\\
\hline
stype & Elementary/Middle/High School\\
\hline
name & School name (15 characters)\\
\hline
sname & School name (40 characters)\\
\hline
snum & School number\\
\hline
dname & District name\\
\hline
dnum & District number\\
\hline
cname & County name\\
\hline
cnum & County number\\
\hline
flag & reason for missing data\\
\hline
pcttest & percentage of students tested\\
\hline
api00 & API in 2000\\
\hline
api99 & API in 1999\\
\hline
target & target for change in API\\
\hline
growth & Change in API\\
\hline
sch.wide & Met school-wide growth target?\\
\hline
comp.imp & Met Comparable Improvement target\\
\hline
both & Met both targets\\
\hline
awards & Eligible for awards program\\
\hline
meals & Percentage of students eligible for subsidized meals\\
\hline
ell & ‘English Language Learners’ (percent)\\
\hline
yr.rnd & Year-round school\\
\hline
mobility & percentage of students for whom this is the first year at the school\\
\hline
acs.k3 & average class size years K-3\\
\hline
acs.46 & average class size years 4-6\\
\hline
acs.core & Number of core academic courses\\
\hline
pct.resp & percent where parental education level is known\\
\hline
not.hsg & percent parents not high-school graduates\\
\hline
hsg & percent parents who are high-school graduates\\
\hline
some.col & percent parents with some college\\
\hline
col.grad & percent parents with college degree\\
\hline
grad.sch & percent parents with postgraduate education\\
\hline
avg.ed & average parental education level\\
\hline
full & percent fully qualified teachers\\
\hline
emer & percent teachers with emergency qualifications\\
\hline
enroll & number of students enrolled\\
\hline
api.stu & number of students tested.\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The \texttt{apiclus2} dataset was drawn by taking a sample of districts (labelled by district number \texttt{dnum}),
and then a sample of schools within districts (schools are labelled by their school number \texttt{snum}).\\
The column \texttt{fpc1} contains the number of districts (757), and the column \texttt{fpc2} contains the
number of schools in each district (which varies by district).

We can specify this two stage cluster design using

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{apiclus2.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{ids=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{dnum}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{snum, }\AttributeTok{fpc=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{fpc1}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{fpc2, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{apiclus2)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The \texttt{summary()} function tells us the basics of the design we've just specified:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(apiclus2.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## 2 - level Cluster Sampling design
## With (40, 126) clusters.
## svydesign(ids = ~dnum + snum, fpc = ~fpc1 + fpc2, data = apiclus2)
## Probabilities:
##     Min.  1st Qu.   Median     Mean  3rd Qu.     Max. 
## 0.003669 0.037743 0.052840 0.042390 0.052840 0.052840 
## Population size (PSUs): 757 
## Data variables:
##  [1] "cds"      "stype"    "name"     "sname"    "snum"     "dname"   
##  [7] "dnum"     "cname"    "cnum"     "flag"     "pcttest"  "api00"   
## [13] "api99"    "target"   "growth"   "sch.wide" "comp.imp" "both"    
## [19] "awards"   "meals"    "ell"      "yr.rnd"   "mobility" "acs.k3"  
## [25] "acs.46"   "acs.core" "pct.resp" "not.hsg"  "hsg"      "some.col"
## [31] "col.grad" "grad.sch" "avg.ed"   "full"     "emer"     "enroll"  
## [37] "api.stu"  "pw"       "fpc1"     "fpc2"
\end{verbatim}

We can see that from the summary that \(n=40\) districts were sampled (out of \(N=757\)).
Let's count the number of schools sampled per district. We'll just display the top 15 largest
districts:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{apiclus2 }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(dnum,dname) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{Mi=}\FunctionTok{first}\NormalTok{(fpc2),}\AttributeTok{mi=}\FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{()) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{ungroup}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{arrange}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{desc}\NormalTok{(Mi)) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{slice\_head}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{15}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{kbl}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{col.names=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"dnum"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"District Name"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"$M\_i$"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"$m\_i$"}\NormalTok{),}\AttributeTok{escape=}\ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{kable\_styling}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{full\_width=}\ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}
