district \(m_i=M_i\)

\section{Continous variables}\label{continous-variables}

Consider the key outcome variable `api00``, the API score in the year
2000 for each school.

\subsection*{Display}\label{display}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Display}

The basic workhorse for the display of numerical variables is the histogram

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svyhist}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{api00, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des, }\AttributeTok{prob=}\ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{,}
        \AttributeTok{xlab=}\StringTok{"API Score (Year 2000)"}\NormalTok{, }
        \AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"Weighted distribution of API (2000) scores"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-147-1} 

}

\caption{Weighted histogram of API (2000) scores}\label{fig:unnamed-chunk-147}
\end{figure}

Note that this histogram differs strongly from the simple histogram
of the sample values:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{hist}\NormalTok{(apiclus2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{api00, }
        \AttributeTok{xlab=}\StringTok{"API Score (Year 2000)"}\NormalTok{, }
        \AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"Unweighted distribution of API (2000) scores"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-148-1} 

}

\caption{Unweighted histogram of API (2000) sample data}\label{fig:unnamed-chunk-148}
\end{figure}

a warning to us that with survey data we can go badly astray if we ignore
the sample design.

Boxplots are an alternative to histograms:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svyboxplot}\NormalTok{(api00}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des, }
           \AttributeTok{ylab=}\StringTok{"API (2000)"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"API (2000) Scores"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-149-1} 

}

\caption{Distribution of API (2000) sample data}\label{fig:unnamed-chunk-149}
\end{figure}

\ldots{} especially if we want to display variables divided by a category:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svyboxplot}\NormalTok{(api00}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des, }
           \AttributeTok{xlab=}\StringTok{"School Type"}\NormalTok{,}
           \AttributeTok{ylab=}\StringTok{"API (2000)"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"API (2000) Scores"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-150-1} 

}

\caption{Distribution of API (2000) sample data}\label{fig:unnamed-chunk-150}
\end{figure}

If we have two continuous variables we can plot them as a scatterplot, showing
the weights of each observation:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svyplot}\NormalTok{(api00}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{api99, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des, }\AttributeTok{style=}\StringTok{"bubble"}\NormalTok{,}
        \AttributeTok{xlab=}\StringTok{"API (1999)"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab=}\StringTok{"API (2000)"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-151-1} 

}

\caption{Distribution of API scores in 1999 and 2000}\label{fig:unnamed-chunk-151}
\end{figure}

\subsection*{Estimation}\label{estimation}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Estimation}

We've already seen that we can estimate means using the \texttt{svymean()} function.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{api00, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##         mean     SE
## api00 670.81 30.099
\end{verbatim}

The \texttt{jtools} package allows us to estimate the population standard deviation too

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(jtools)}
\FunctionTok{svysd}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{api00, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##       std. dev.
## api00    136.83
\end{verbatim}

(\textbf{Pause a minute} and remind yourself of the difference between a standard deviation and
a standard error.)

We can estimates means for a set of separate variables, using a perhaps uninintuitive notation:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{api99}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{api00, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##         mean     SE
## api99 645.03 29.711
## api00 670.81 30.099
\end{verbatim}

or for a variable split by categories by wrapping \texttt{svymean()} up inside a call to \texttt{svyby()}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svyby}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{api00, }\AttributeTok{by=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, svymean, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##                 stype    api00       se
## Elementary Elementary 692.8104 29.92660
## Middle         Middle 642.3520 45.09132
## High             High 598.3407 17.69417
\end{verbatim}

A \textbf{dotchart} is a nice way of showing the estimated means and their confidence intervals:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ss }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svyby}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{api00, }\AttributeTok{by=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, svymean, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des)}
\NormalTok{sc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{confint}\NormalTok{(ss)}
\NormalTok{ns }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(sc)}
\FunctionTok{dotchart}\NormalTok{(ss, }\AttributeTok{xlim=}\FunctionTok{range}\NormalTok{(sc), }\AttributeTok{xlab=}\StringTok{"Mean API (2000) Score"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{arrows}\NormalTok{(sc[,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], }\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{ns, sc[,}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{], }\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{ns, }\AttributeTok{angle=}\DecValTok{90}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{code=}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length=}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-156-1} 

}

\caption{Mean API (2000) scores by School Type}\label{fig:unnamed-chunk-156}
\end{figure}

If we want to test statistical hypotheses about differences between groups
one way is to use the regression framework

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{svyglm}\NormalTok{(api00}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## 
## Call:
## svyglm(formula = api00 ~ stype, design = apiclus2.des)
## 
## Survey design:
## svydesign(ids = ~dnum + snum, fpc = ~fpc1 + fpc2, data = apiclus2)
## 
## Coefficients:
##             Estimate Std. Error t value Pr(>|t|)    
## (Intercept)   692.81      29.93  23.150  < 2e-16 ***
## stypeMiddle   -50.46      25.30  -1.994  0.05354 .  
## stypeHigh     -94.47      28.21  -3.348  0.00188 ** 
## ---
## Signif. codes:  0 '***' 0.001 '**' 0.01 '*' 0.05 '.' 0.1 ' ' 1
## 
## (Dispersion parameter for gaussian family taken to be 17528.44)
## 
## Number of Fisher Scoring iterations: 2
\end{verbatim}

Here we can see that with Elementary as the reference level we
get the same estimate of the mean API reported as the \texttt{(Intercept)}.
We further see that there is no significant difference between Middle
and Elementary Schools in mean API, but the mean API is significantly lower
at High Schools compared to Elementary Schools.

Regressions take of course take lots of variables into account, and
we can apply familiar regression techniques to investigating
conditional associations between predictors and a continuous outcome.

For example we can test whether the percentage of children at a school
who have free school meals (i.e.~the \texttt{meals} variable) associates with the
API score:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{svyglm}\NormalTok{(api00}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{meals, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## 
## Call:
## svyglm(formula = api00 ~ stype + meals, design = apiclus2.des)
## 
## Survey design:
## svydesign(ids = ~dnum + snum, fpc = ~fpc1 + fpc2, data = apiclus2)
## 
## Coefficients:
##              Estimate Std. Error t value Pr(>|t|)    
## (Intercept)  874.8638    16.4677  53.126  < 2e-16 ***
## stypeMiddle  -60.9517    20.6964  -2.945  0.00563 ** 
## stypeHigh   -169.2128    20.2618  -8.351 6.06e-10 ***
## meals         -3.2367     0.2888 -11.209 2.70e-13 ***
## ---
## Signif. codes:  0 '***' 0.001 '**' 0.01 '*' 0.05 '.' 0.1 ' ' 1
## 
## (Dispersion parameter for gaussian family taken to be 5560.106)
## 
## Number of Fisher Scoring iterations: 2
\end{verbatim}

\ldots{} and there is indeed a strong negative association: the higher the
\texttt{meals} value the lower the API score.

We can estimate \textbf{totals} using the \texttt{svytotal()} function - for example
we can estimate the total number of students enrolled at all schools in
California:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svytotal}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{enroll, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des, }\AttributeTok{na.rm=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          total     SE
## enroll 2639273 799638
\end{verbatim}

Although note that we have had to remove some schools with unknown
\texttt{enroll} values by stating \texttt{na.rm=TRUE}, and these forced removals likely mean
that our estimate of the total is an underestimate.

Confidence intervals can be calculated using the \texttt{confint()} function

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{confint}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{api99}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{api00, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des), }\AttributeTok{level=}\FloatTok{0.95}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          2.5 %   97.5 %
## api99 586.8009 703.2670
## api00 611.8188 729.8048
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{confint}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{svyby}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{api00, }\AttributeTok{by=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, svymean, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##               2.5 %   97.5 %
## Elementary 634.1553 751.4655
## Middle     553.9746 730.7294
## High       563.6607 633.0206
\end{verbatim}

The standard errors can be directly extracted using \texttt{SE()}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{SE}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{svyby}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{api00, }\AttributeTok{by=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, svymean, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## [1] 29.92660 45.09132 17.69417
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Relative Sampling Errors (RSEs)}\label{relative-sampling-errors-rses}

The \textbf{relative standard errors}, also known as \textbf{relative sampling errors}
(RSEs) can be extracted by \texttt{cv()}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{cv}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{svyby}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{api00, }\AttributeTok{by=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, svymean, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## Elementary     Middle       High 
## 0.04319595 0.07019721 0.02957206
\end{verbatim}

RSEs are a useful measure of quality of estimates of quantities that are
strictly positive, such as the API score.

If we learned that the SE of an estimate (e.g.~the mean of \texttt{api00}) was
30.1 we wouldn't be able to assess whether it
was a good estimate or not without knowing that the value of the estimate
was 670.81. A good way to assess the quality of
an estimate is the SE of an estimate \(\widehat{T}\) as a proportion of
the estimate \(\widehat{T}\):
\[
   \bfa{RSE}{\widehat{T}} = \frac{\bfa{SE}{\widehat{T}}}{\widehat{T}}
\]
For overall mean \texttt{api00} the RSE is
\[
   \bfa{RSE}{\widehat{\bar{Y}}} 
   = \frac{30.1}{670.81} 
   = 0.045
\]
This is a highly precise estimate: the SE is only 4.5\%
of the value of the estimate.

Good RSEs are only a few percent at most. Poor RSEs are larger than 10\%, and anything bigger
than 20\% isn't really publishable.

In general a standard deviation divided by a mean goes by the term
\textbf{coefficient of variation} (CV), which is why the R function is
called \texttt{cv()}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{cv}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{api00, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##            api00
## api00 0.04486956
\end{verbatim}

\section{Categorical Variables}\label{categorical-variables}

The workhorse display for categorical variables is the barplot,
showing \textbf{proportions} in categories. Proportions are first calculated
using the \texttt{svymean()} function:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{barplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des),}
        \AttributeTok{names=}\FunctionTok{levels}\NormalTok{(apiclus2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{stype),}
        \AttributeTok{xlab=}\StringTok{"School Type"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab=}\StringTok{"Proportion"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-166-1} 

}

\caption{Estimates of the proportions of Schools of each type}\label{fig:unnamed-chunk-166}
\end{figure}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##                    mean     SE
## stypeElementary 0.68118 0.0556
## stypeMiddle     0.18450 0.0222
## stypeHigh       0.13432 0.0567
\end{verbatim}

It's worth pausing to recognise that a proportion is a mean of a special
type of variable called an \textbf{indicator variable}: it takes the value 1 if
the observation is a member of specific category and 0 if not. Add up all the ones
and zeroes and you get the number of observations in the category, and divide
by the number of observations you get a proportion.
\[
    p = \frac{1 + 0 + 1 + 1 + 0 + 0 + ....}{n} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n Y_i
\]
where \(Y_i\) is an indicator variable.

With sample data we calculate \textbf{weighted sums} though:
\[
   \widehat{p} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^n w_i Y_i}{\sum_{i=1}^n w_i} = \frac{\widehat{Y}}{\widehat{N}}
\]
where the top line of this fraction is \(\widehat{Y}\), our estimate of the
number of individuals (in a finite population) who belong to the category of
interest. The bottom line is \(\widehat{N}\), the estimated size of the population.
\(\widehat{N}\) is exact in some designs (e.g.~SRSWOR, or Stratified SRSWOR), but
is approximate in other designs (especially cluster designs).

These total estimates within categories can be estimated using the \texttt{svytotal()}
function.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svytotal}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##                   total      SE
## stypeElementary 3493.56 1119.75
## stypeMiddle      946.25  311.81
## stypeHigh        688.87  289.35
\end{verbatim}

When creating barplots of estimated proportions and/or totals
we really should indicate the uncertainties in our
estimates, and show confidence intervals on the bars. We can use the standard
errors provided by \texttt{svymean()} and \texttt{svytotal()}: the function \texttt{confint()}
will provide these:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{confint}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{svytotal}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##                     2.5 %   97.5 %
## stypeElementary 1298.8892 5688.221
## stypeMiddle      335.1077 1557.392
## stypeHigh        121.7472 1255.993
\end{verbatim}

These confidence intervals are symmetric about the point estimates:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ss }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des)}
\NormalTok{ci }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{confint}\NormalTok{(ss)}
\NormalTok{bp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{barplot}\NormalTok{(ss, }\AttributeTok{xlab=}\StringTok{"School Type"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab=}\StringTok{"Estimated Size"}\NormalTok{,}
              \AttributeTok{names=}\FunctionTok{levels}\NormalTok{(apiclus2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{stype),}
              \AttributeTok{ylim=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}\FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(ci)))}
\FunctionTok{arrows}\NormalTok{(bp, ci[,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], bp, ci[,}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{angle=}\DecValTok{90}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length=}\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{code=}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-170-1} 

}

\caption{Estimated proportions of schools by school type - with symmetric confidence intervals}\label{fig:unnamed-chunk-170}
\end{figure}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{names}\NormalTok{(ss) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{levels}\NormalTok{(apiclus2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{stype)}
\FunctionTok{cbind}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{as.numeric}\NormalTok{(ss),ci) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{kbl}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{col.names=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Estimate"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"2.5\%"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"97.5\%"}\NormalTok{), }
      \AttributeTok{digits=}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{,}
      \AttributeTok{caption=}\StringTok{"Symmetric confidence interval estimates"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{kable\_styling}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{full\_width=}\ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\centering
\caption{(\#tab:tabci.symmetric)Symmetric confidence interval estimates}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|r|r|r}
\hline
  & Estimate & 2.5\% & 97.5\%\\
\hline
stypeElementary & 0.681 & 0.572 & 0.790\\
\hline
stypeMiddle & 0.185 & 0.141 & 0.228\\
\hline
stypeHigh & 0.134 & 0.023 & 0.246\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

These symmetric intervals are actually somewhat unrealistic, because
\texttt{confint()} doesn't know it's dealing with indicator variables, and
doesn't take into account the fact that proportions and totals can't go
negative.

A better method of constructing confidence intervals is the special
function \texttt{svyciprop()}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svyciprop}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\FunctionTok{I}\NormalTok{(stype}\SpecialCharTok{==}\StringTok{"Elementary"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##                                 2.5% 97.5%
## I(stype == "Elementary") 0.681 0.560 0.782
\end{verbatim}

Note that we've had to create an indicator variable here using the \texttt{I()}
function for just one level in order to calculate its confidence interval.

To get a confidence interval proportion for every level of a factor
it's somewhat fiddly with the raw \texttt{survey} package:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ss }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des)}
\NormalTok{ci }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{t}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sapply}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{levels}\NormalTok{(apiclus2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{stype),}
       \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(stypelevel) \{}
\NormalTok{          form }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{eval}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{parse}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{text=}\FunctionTok{paste0}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"formula(\textasciitilde{}I(stype==}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}"}\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{,stypelevel,}\StringTok{"}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}"}\StringTok{))"}\NormalTok{)))}
          \FunctionTok{confint}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{svyciprop}\NormalTok{(form, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des))    }
\NormalTok{       \}))}
\NormalTok{bp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{barplot}\NormalTok{(ss, }\AttributeTok{xlab=}\StringTok{"School Type"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab=}\StringTok{"Estimated Size"}\NormalTok{,}
              \AttributeTok{names=}\FunctionTok{levels}\NormalTok{(apiclus2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{stype),}
              \AttributeTok{ylim=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}\FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(ci)))}
\FunctionTok{arrows}\NormalTok{(bp, ci[,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], bp, ci[,}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{angle=}\DecValTok{90}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length=}\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{code=}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-172-1} 

}

\caption{Estimated proportions of schools by school type - with asymmetric confidence intervals}\label{fig:unnamed-chunk-172}
\end{figure}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{names}\NormalTok{(ss) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{levels}\NormalTok{(apiclus2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{stype)}
\FunctionTok{cbind}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{as.numeric}\NormalTok{(ss),ci) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{kbl}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{col.names=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Estimate"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"2.5\%"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"97.5\%"}\NormalTok{), }
      \AttributeTok{digits=}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{,}
      \AttributeTok{caption=}\StringTok{"Asymmetric confidence interval estimates"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{kable\_styling}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{full\_width=}\ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\centering
\caption{(\#tab:tabci.asymmetric)Asymmetric confidence interval estimates}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|r|r|r}
\hline
  & Estimate & 2.5\% & 97.5\%\\
\hline
Elementary & 0.681 & 0.560 & 0.782\\
\hline
Middle & 0.185 & 0.144 & 0.234\\
\hline
High & 0.134 & 0.055 & 0.294\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The biggest thing to notice is that the confidence interval for ``High''
is now asymmetric: it's longer on the high side that the low side, and
more correctly represents our uncertainty. The estimates for ``Elementary''
and ``Middle'' are essentially unchanged.

For \textbf{crosstabulations} we can use \texttt{svytable()}, which gives weighted
counts in categories - e.g.~of whether School Wide growth targest had been met

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svytable}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{sch.wide, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##             sch.wide
## stype              No      Yes
##   Elementary  242.240 3251.315
##   Middle      446.630  499.620
##   High        586.675  102.195
\end{verbatim}

Which can be displayed with a side-by-side barplot

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{st }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svytable}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{sch.wide}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{stype, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des)}
\NormalTok{bp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{barplot}\NormalTok{(st, }\AttributeTok{beside=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"light blue"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"dark red"}\NormalTok{),}
              \AttributeTok{legend=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{args.legend=}\FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{title=}\StringTok{"Met targets"}\NormalTok{), }
              \AttributeTok{xlab=}\StringTok{"School Type"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab=}\StringTok{"Frequency"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-174-1} 

}

\caption{Counts of schools meeting targets, by school type}\label{fig:unnamed-chunk-174}
\end{figure}

A test of association is given by the weighted Chi-Square test

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svychisq}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{sch.wide, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## 
##  Pearson's X^2: Rao & Scott adjustment
## 
## data:  svychisq(~stype + sch.wide, design = apiclus2.des)
## F = 11.873, ndf = 1.3825, ddf = 53.9175, p-value = 0.0003221
\end{verbatim}

We can carry out a logistic regression to make a similar test of association.\\
We have \texttt{sch.wide} as the binary outcome variable and \texttt{stype} as the predictor:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{svyglm}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{I}\NormalTok{(sch.wide}\SpecialCharTok{==}\StringTok{"Yes"}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, }\AttributeTok{family=}\FunctionTok{quasibinomial}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{link=}\StringTok{"logit"}\NormalTok{), }
               \AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## 
## Call:
## svyglm(formula = I(sch.wide == "Yes") ~ stype, design = apiclus2.des, 
##     family = quasibinomial(link = "logit"))
## 
## Survey design:
## svydesign(ids = ~dnum + snum, fpc = ~fpc1 + fpc2, data = apiclus2)
## 
## Coefficients:
##             Estimate Std. Error t value Pr(>|t|)    
## (Intercept)   2.5969     0.5997   4.331 0.000109 ***
## stypeMiddle  -2.4848     1.1565  -2.148 0.038292 *  
## stypeHigh    -4.3445     0.8757  -4.961 1.59e-05 ***
## ---
## Signif. codes:  0 '***' 0.001 '**' 0.01 '*' 0.05 '.' 0.1 ' ' 1
## 
## (Dispersion parameter for quasibinomial family taken to be 1.008)
## 
## Number of Fisher Scoring iterations: 5
\end{verbatim}

With regular \texttt{lm()} and \texttt{glm()} regression fits we can use F tests to determine
whether specific terms in the model should be included. A similar test
can be done to test for the inclusion of specific terms using \texttt{regTermTest}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{sr }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svyglm}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{I}\NormalTok{(sch.wide}\SpecialCharTok{==}\StringTok{"Yes"}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, }\AttributeTok{family=}\FunctionTok{quasibinomial}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{link=}\StringTok{"logit"}\NormalTok{), }
               \AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apiclus2.des)}
\FunctionTok{regTermTest}\NormalTok{(sr, }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## Wald test for stype
##  in svyglm(formula = I(sch.wide == "Yes") ~ stype, design = apiclus2.des, 
##     family = quasibinomial(link = "logit"))
## F =  13.61363  on  2  and  37  df: p= 3.7061e-05
\end{verbatim}

Here we can see that \texttt{stype} is contributing significantly to the fit of the model.

\section{Ratio estimation}\label{ratio-estimation}

There often arise circumstances in survey analysis where we can expect one variable
to be proportional to another: for example population and GDP are likely to move together.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-179-1} 

}

\caption{Population and GDP (2023) (Source: Our World in Data)}\label{fig:unnamed-chunk-179}
\end{figure}

The are three clear outliers here are the US, China and India.

We have full data on \(N=150\) countries, and the total GDP of the world
in 2023 was \(Y=\ensuremath{9.1046\times 10^{4}}\) in billions of US dollars.

Let's imagine though that we only had a sample of \(n=40\) countries out of the
\(N=150\) and wanted to estimate the total GDP \(Y\). The first 10 rows of our sample
are shown below.

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|r|r|r|r}
\hline
Country & Population & GDP & bigN & weight\\
\hline
Zambia & 20.723967 & 27.580696 & 150 & 3.75\\
\hline
Saudi Arabia & 33.264289 & 863.963569 & 150 & 3.75\\
\hline
Mauritius & 1.273591 & 14.170188 & 150 & 3.75\\
\hline
Bahrain & 1.569674 & 39.966811 & 150 & 3.75\\
\hline
Benin & 14.111035 & 17.831007 & 150 & 3.75\\
\hline
Myanmar & 54.133800 & 63.756974 & 150 & 3.75\\
\hline
Mozambique & 33.635163 & 20.532020 & 150 & 3.75\\
\hline
Bosnia and Herzegovina & 3.185072 & 20.681711 & 150 & 3.75\\
\hline
Montenegro & 0.633553 & 5.161591 & 150 & 3.75\\
\hline
Japan & 124.370947 & 4601.203386 & 150 & 3.75\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-181-1} 

}

\caption{Sample of 40 countries: Population and GDP (2023) (Source: Our World in Data)}\label{fig:unnamed-chunk-181}
\end{figure}

Our sample happens to miss out on all three of the big countries here.
Note that there are some mid-size countries with low GDP visible in the
plot, so that the relationship between population and GDP isn't simple.

Our design specification is:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{gdp.sample.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{id=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fpc=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{bigN, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{gdp.sample)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

and our total GDP estimate is

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ss1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svytotal}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{GDP, gdp.sample.des)}
\NormalTok{ss1}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##     total    SE
## GDP 81890 22980
\end{verbatim}

This estimate has a very high SE due to the high variability among
countries in GDP, and leads to a very wide confidence interval

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ci1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{confint}\NormalTok{(ss1)}
\NormalTok{ci1}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##        2.5 %   97.5 %
## GDP 36850.06 126930.8
\end{verbatim}

with a very high RSE

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{cv}\NormalTok{(ss1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##           GDP
## GDP 0.2806213
\end{verbatim}

indicating an estimate of low quality.

We could use the correlation between population \(x_i\) and GDP \(y_i\) and
fit a standard regression model
\[
   y_i = \beta x_i + \varepsilon_i\qquad \varepsilon_i\sim N(0,\sigma^2)
\]
omitting the intercept since we want GDP to be proportional to population.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{lmfit }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svyglm}\NormalTok{(GDP }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ Population, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{gdp.sample.des)}
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(lmfit)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## 
## Call:
## svyglm(formula = GDP ~ -1 + Population, design = gdp.sample.des)
## 
## Survey design:
## svydesign(id = ~1, fpc = ~bigN, data = gdp.sample)
## 
## Coefficients:
##            Estimate Std. Error t value Pr(>|t|)    
## Population   27.870      4.117    6.77 4.44e-08 ***
## ---
## Signif. codes:  0 '***' 0.001 '**' 0.01 '*' 0.05 '.' 0.1 ' ' 1
## 
## (Dispersion parameter for gaussian family taken to be 423229.9)
## 
## Number of Fisher Scoring iterations: 2
\end{verbatim}

The coefficient \(\beta\) is the GDP (\$Billions) per million people in the population.
A 95\% confidence interval for \(\beta\) is

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{confint}\NormalTok{(lmfit)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##               2.5 %   97.5 %
## Population 19.54262 36.19642
\end{verbatim}

It does have a better RSE than our earlier estimate

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{cv}\NormalTok{(lmfit)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## Population 
##  0.1477149
\end{verbatim}

Scaling the estimated GDP per million by the total population of the world (\(X=7646.716928\) million) we
still get a rather wide confidence interval

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ci2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{confint}\NormalTok{(lmfit)}\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{poptotal}
\NormalTok{ci2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##               2.5 %   97.5 %
## Population 149436.9 276783.8
\end{verbatim}

The reason we're still not doing very well is that the regression model isn't a good representation of
the situation. Take a look at the residual plot:

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-190-1} 

}

\caption{Residual plot}\label{fig:unnamed-chunk-190}
\end{figure}

This is a terrible looking residual plot, not least because the variance
so clearly isn't constant: there is clear \textbf{funnelling} of the residuals.
The variance is increasing with population. That isn't surprising in that
there is enormous variety in GDP per capita among the larger countries.

An improvement on the model is to have a residual variance which
scales with population \(x_i\):
\[
   y_i = \beta x_i + \varepsilon_i\qquad \varepsilon_i\sim N(0,\sigma^2x_i)
\]
We can estimate this ratio of the total of \(Y\) to the total of \(X\)
directly using the \texttt{svyratio()} function:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ss3 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svyratio}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{GDP, }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Population, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{gdp.sample.des)}
\NormalTok{ss3}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## Ratio estimator: svyratio.survey.design2(~GDP, ~Population, design = gdp.sample.des)
## Ratios=
##     Population
## GDP   21.55548
## SEs=
##     Population
## GDP   3.809595
\end{verbatim}

The SE is slightly larger than in our first regression model, but we have more
confidence in this model due to its better treatment of the variance. The confidence
interval for the GDP per capita is now

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{confint}\NormalTok{(ss3)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##                   2.5 %   97.5 %
## GDP/Population 14.08881 29.02215
\end{verbatim}

leading to the following confidence interval for the total GDP of the world in 2023:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ci3 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{confint}\NormalTok{(ss3)}\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{poptotal}
\NormalTok{ci3}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##                   2.5 %   97.5 %
## GDP/Population 107733.2 221924.2
\end{verbatim}

Collecting all of these results together we see the improvement in our estimate
by using the additional information of population size alongside each country's GDP.

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|r|r|l}
\hline
Method & Estimate & RSE & CI\\
\hline
Simple & 81890 & 0.28 & (36850,126931)\\
\hline
Regression & 213110 & 0.15 & (149437,276784)\\
\hline
Ratio & 164829 & 0.18 & (107733,221924)\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\chapter{Weight adjustments}\label{weight-adjustments}

\section{Weight Calibration}\label{weight-calibration}

At the end of a survey we may find ourselves in the undesirable situation that

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Purely by chance}, the sample is somewhat unbalanced: e.g.~by chance we may have
  selected many more males than females, or more large households than small ones, or
\item
  We may have had \textbf{unit nonresponse} - i.e.~there may be selected units from
  which we have collected no data. In the case of human subjects this may have
  been from a failure to contact the selected person, or the refusal of the
  person to answer our questions.
\end{itemize}

In both cases we are in the situation that our sample, even when weighted, is
not a good representation of the population from which it is drawn.

For example consider the SURF dataset, which is a synthetic sample of 200 individuals
between the ages of 15 and 45. The first 10 rows are shown below.

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{r|l|l|r|r|r|l|l|l|r|r}
\hline
Personid & Gender & Qualification & Age & Hours & Income & Marital & Ethnicity & AgeGrp & bigN & weight\\
\hline
1 & female & school & 15 & 4 & 87 & never & European & 15-24 & 1972719 & 9863.595\\
\hline
2 & female & vocational & 40 & 42 & 596 & married & European & 35-45 & 1972719 & 9863.595\\
\hline
3 & male & none & 38 & 40 & 497 & married & Maori & 35-45 & 1972719 & 9863.595\\
\hline
4 & female & vocational & 34 & 8 & 299 & never & European & 25-34 & 1972719 & 9863.595\\
\hline
5 & female & school & 45 & 16 & 301 & married & European & 35-45 & 1972719 & 9863.595\\
\hline
6 & male & degree & 45 & 50 & 1614 & married & European & 35-45 & 1972719 & 9863.595\\
\hline
7 & female & none & 36 & 12 & 201 & other & European & 35-45 & 1972719 & 9863.595\\
\hline
8 & male & degree & 35 & 45 & 934 & previously & European & 35-45 & 1972719 & 9863.595\\
\hline
9 & female & vocational & 38 & 26 & 624 & married & European & 35-45 & 1972719 & 9863.595\\
\hline
10 & male & school & 37 & 50 & 533 & previously & European & 35-45 & 1972719 & 9863.595\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The dataset contains a mixture of numerical and categorical variables, all with very
self explanatory names (except note that \texttt{Income} and \texttt{Hours} are weekly quantities,
and also that the income values are somewhat old!). We'll treat it as a SRSWOR from
the New Zealand population between the ages of 15 and 45.

The total population in this age range is \(N=\ensuremath{1.972719\times 10^{6}}\), the sample size is \(n=200\),
and so the weight of each sampled individual is
\[
   w = \frac{N}{n} = {\ensuremath{1.972719\times 10^{6}}}{200} = 9863.6
\]
Let's specify this simple random sampling design to R:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{surf.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{id=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fpc=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{bigN, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{surf)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

and then estimate the mean income:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Income, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{surf.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean     SE
## Income 575.36 24.508
\end{verbatim}

We should note that the mean income differs considerably between males and females.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svyby}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Income, }\AttributeTok{by=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Gender, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{surf.des, svymean)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##        Gender   Income       se
## male     male 732.8172 37.02642
## female female 438.5047 26.16272
\end{verbatim}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{_main_files/_main_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-201-1.pdf}}

Let's also note the distribution of the population by age and sex, as measured
by the most recent census:

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|r|r|r}
\hline
AgeGrp & male & female & Total\\
\hline
15-24 & 324888 & 310032 & 634920\\
\hline
25-34 & 337845 & 340668 & 678513\\
\hline
35-45 & 321358 & 337928 & 659286\\
\hline
Total & 984091 & 988628 & 1972719\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Or we can express these counts as percentages:

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|l|l|l}
\hline
AgeGrp & male & female & Total\\
\hline
15-24 & 16.5\% & 15.7\% & 32.2\%\\
\hline
25-34 & 17.1\% & 17.3\% & 34.4\%\\
\hline
35-45 & 16.3\% & 17.1\% & 33.4\%\\
\hline
Total & 49.9\% & 50.1\% & 100.0\%\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The proportions of males and females in the population are roughly equal in the population,
but in the sample they're not quite equal:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Gender, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{surf.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##               mean     SE
## Gendermale   0.465 0.0354
## Genderfemale 0.535 0.0354
\end{verbatim}

This has been caused by our having, by chance, sampled slightly more females than males.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{table}\NormalTok{(surf}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Gender)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## 
##   male female 
##     93    107
\end{verbatim}

The consequence of this is that our estimate of the population mean income is
and underestimate, due to females earning less than males, and their being
slightly overrepresented in the sample.

One obvious solution to this situation is to slightly upweight the males in the sample, and downweight
the females, to take care of the imbalance. Of course we only \textbf{know} about the
imbalance because we have the census counts which tell us that the proportions of
males and females are roughly equal: otherwise we'd have no idea that there might be a problem.

These external counts (external to the survey that is) are called \textbf{population benchmarks},
and the weight adjustment that makes the weights match the benchmarks is called
\textbf{poststratification}. It's `post-' here because we're making the stratification
(breaking into groups) \textbf{after} the survey has taken place. Breaking the sample into
groups \textbf{beforehand}, and taking separate samples within each group is called stratified
sampling. But that is only possible if we know the group membership of sample members before
we sample them - i.e.~if the group membership information is available on the frame.

We have \(n=200\) individuals in a SRSWOR drawn from a population
of size \(N=\ensuremath{1.972719\times 10^{6}}\), with weights \(w=\ensuremath{1.972719\times 10^{6}}/200=9863.6\). These
correctly add up to the population total
\[
   n \times w = n \times \frac{N}{n} = 200\times 9863.6 = \ensuremath{1.972719\times 10^{6}}
\]
What we want is different weights for the males and females, so that they separately
add up to the total numbers of males \(N_1=\ensuremath{9.84091\times 10^{5}}\) and the total
numbers of females \(N_2=\ensuremath{9.88628\times 10^{5}}\) in the population. We can achieve this
by computing new post-stratified weights using the known benchmarks \(N_h\) and sample
sizes \(n_h\) in each of the post-strata, and computing new weights
\[
   w_h = \frac{N_h}{n_h}
\]
i.e.~these are the weights we'd have used if we had actually carried out a
stratified sample in the first place.

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|r|r|r|r}
\hline
Gender & SampWeight & nh & Nh & PostStratWeight\\
\hline
male & 9863.595 & 93 & 984091 & 10581.624\\
\hline
female & 9863.595 & 107 & 988628 & 9239.514\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

These new weights are now appropriately higher for males and lower for females.

In R we can modify the weights in the design object using the \texttt{postStratify()} function.
We first need to create a data frame storing the benchmarks:

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|r}
\hline
Gender & Nh\\
\hline
male & 984091\\
\hline
female & 988628\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{surf.des.ps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{postStratify}\NormalTok{(surf.des, }\AttributeTok{strata=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Gender, }\AttributeTok{population=}\NormalTok{Gender.benchmarks)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The orignal estimate of the proportions of males and females

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Gender, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{surf.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##               mean     SE
## Gendermale   0.465 0.0354
## Genderfemale 0.535 0.0354
\end{verbatim}

has been modified to

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Gender, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{surf.des.ps)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##                 mean SE
## Gendermale   0.49885  0
## Genderfemale 0.50115  0
\end{verbatim}

Notice that the Standard Errors here are zero: these proportions are no longer
estimates, they have been \textbf{imposed} by our benchmarks.

Then we can compare the orginal estimate of mean income

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Income, surf.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean     SE
## Income 575.36 24.508
\end{verbatim}

with the post-stratified estimate

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Income, surf.des.ps)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean     SE
## Income 585.32 22.651
\end{verbatim}

The estimate has been increased slightly as expected. Also note that
the Standard Error has been reduced: this is often the case with
post-stratified estimates: post-stratification makes the properties
of different samples differ less than they otherwise would, and thereby
reduces our overall uncertainty.

\subsection*{Poststratfication as a treatment for non-response}\label{poststratfication-as-a-treatment-for-non-response}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Poststratfication as a treatment for non-response}

Non-response can make the small chance differences in the previous section even worse.

In population surveys males and young people tend to have the lowest response rates.
So let's see what happens to our mean income estimate if we take our sample of 200 individuals
and filter out 20\% of the male respondents:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{234}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{surf.nonresp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ surf}
\NormalTok{surf.nonresp}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Responds }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rbinom}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(surf),}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}\AttributeTok{prob=}\FunctionTok{ifelse}\NormalTok{(surf}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Gender}\SpecialCharTok{==}\StringTok{"male"}\NormalTok{,}\FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{,}\FloatTok{1.0}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{surf.nonresp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ surf.nonresp[surf.nonresp}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Responds}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,]}
\NormalTok{surf.nonresp}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{weight }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ bigN}\SpecialCharTok{/}\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(surf.nonresp)}
\FunctionTok{table}\NormalTok{(surf.nonresp}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Gender)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## 
##   male female 
##     75    107
\end{verbatim}

We've now got a lot fewer males than females. Let's see what this does to the
simple estimate of the population mean income:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{surf.nonresp.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{id=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fpc=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{bigN, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{surf.nonresp)}
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Income, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{surf.nonresp.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean     SE
## Income 555.99 24.501
\end{verbatim}

The introduction of the non-response has led to a reduction in our original estimate
from 575.36 to 555.99.

Post-stratifying however

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{surf.nonresp.des.ps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{postStratify}\NormalTok{(surf.nonresp.des, }\AttributeTok{strata=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Gender, }
                                    \AttributeTok{population=}\NormalTok{Gender.benchmarks)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

leads to a dramatically improved estimate

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Income, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{surf.nonresp.des.ps)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean     SE
## Income 580.73 23.354
\end{verbatim}

We know more than just the distribution of the population over Gender, we also know it over the
three age groups. So we can post-stratify by these variables too.

We can store the AgeGrp/Gender benchmarks in a data frame \texttt{AgeGrp.Gender.benchmarks}:

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|l|r}
\hline
AgeGrp & Gender & count\\
\hline
15-24 & female & 310032\\
\hline
15-24 & male & 324888\\
\hline
25-34 & female & 340668\\
\hline
25-34 & male & 337845\\
\hline
35-45 & female & 337928\\
\hline
35-45 & male & 321358\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

and then post-stratify

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{surf.nonresp.des.ps2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{postStratify}\NormalTok{(surf.nonresp.des, }\AttributeTok{strata=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{AgeGrp}\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{Gender, }
                                     \AttributeTok{population=}\NormalTok{AgeGrp.Gender.benchmarks)}
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Income, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{surf.nonresp.des.ps2)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean    SE
## Income 580.26 22.66
\end{verbatim}

And this is estimate is going to be even better than before.

However where should we stop? Should we post-stratify by as many variables as we
possibly can?

In general we need to ensure that we have enough individuals in the sample
in every cross-classified cell (in this case AgeGroup by Gender) to be able to
recalculate the weight. Empty cells are going to cause us a problem,
since we'll be discarding the population weight associated
with people of that type in the population who happen not to have turned up in the sample.

In population surveys run by Statistics NZ we'll often see post-stratification by age
(in 5 year bands), sex, ethnicity (in large amalgamated groups), and large regions.

\subsubsection*{The origin and nature of nonresponse}\label{the-origin-and-nature-of-nonresponse}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{The origin and nature of nonresponse}

Post-stratification is often used to modify weights in the presence of non-response.
The idea is that in each post-stratum the people who respond represent well the people
who don't respond.

This is a very big and untestable assumption.

The reasons that people do or don't respond are complicated. The greatest risk to a
survey is that the non-respondents differ from the respondents on the characteristic
of interest.

For example if we're doing a survey of incomes, perhaps all the high income
people don't respond - there's no way to adjust for this, because the only way to know about
income is to have them answer the survey. So we'll never find out about high income people,
nor find out what their incomes actually are.

Post-stratifying the income survey above by age and sex means that we're assuming that
the respondents in each age-sex category are a simple random sample from all of the
sample members selected into the survey. We can cope with, and adjust for, differing response rates
\textbf{between} the post-strata, but we can never adjust for different likelihoods of response
\textbf{within} the post-strata.

A helpful approach to missing data has a typology with three types of missingness.\\
If we have auxliary data \({\bf X}_i\) for each unit, and are interested in an outcome
variable \(Y_i\) for each unit, then we are interested in the \textbf{probability that unit \(i\)
will respond if selected}
\[
   \phi_i = \Pr(\text{Unit $i$ responds} | \text{Unit $i$ is selected})
\]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Missing Completely at Random (MCAR)}. This is when the probability of
  response \(\phi_i\) is the same for all units, no matter what their value of
  \(Y_i\) and \({\bf X}_i\).

  This is the best situation: the respondents and nonrespondents do not
  differ in any important way, and it is as if the nonrespondents had been
  selected at random from the sample. To analyse data where we have MCAR,
  we can simply ignore the non-responding units.
\item
  \textbf{Missing at Random (MAR)}. This is also known as `missing at random given
  covariates' or alternatively `ignorable nonresponse.' This occurs when
  the probability of response \(\phi_i\) depends \textbf{only on known quantities}:
  the auxiliary covariates \({\bf X}_i\), but not on the unknown \(Y_i\).
  We therefore assume that if two units have the same values of \({\bf X}\), then their
  likelihood of response is the same.

  This is a slightly more complex situation than MCAR, but we can still
  fully account for nonresponse. This is the approach that post-stratification
  implements: we treat all respondents as equally likely to respond within
  the post-strata.
\item
  \textbf{Nonignorable Nonresponse.} This is the unfortunate situation where the
  probability of response \(\phi_i\) depends on the outcome of interest \(Y_i\)
  (as well, possibly, as \({\bf X}_i\)).
\end{enumerate}

MAR and MCAR are \textbf{strong assumptions}. If the data are MAR we can test whether
they are MCAR by comparing nonresponse rates in subgroups defined by \({\bf X}\) (or by
logistic regression if any of the X variables are continuous) and test for
any dependence on \({\bf X}\).

However it is in general impossible to distinguish whether the data are MAR or
whether there is nonignorable response.

We of course exclude from consideration any data which is \textbf{missing by design.}
There are individuals who if selected turn out to be out of scope (ineligible to
participate). These may also be data items which are missing because the respondent
is not asked that particular question (e.g.~we do not ask for the voting
record of children who are not entitled to vote.)

\subsection*{Raking}\label{raking}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Raking}

We may not have full cross-classified benchmarks, for example we might know the
totals by AgeGrp and totals by Gender, but not totals by AgeGrp and Gender simultaneously.
In that case we can proceed iteratively: we post-stratify first by one variable, and
then by other. Each post-stratification on one variable messes up the post-stratification
on the other. But eventually the weights will converge to approximately match both sets
of benchmarks. This iteration is called \textbf{raking} (like raking a lawn one way, then other other),
or \textbf{Iterative Proportional Fitting}.

R implements raking in with the \texttt{rake()} function. We need a separate set of benchmarks
for AgeGrp

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|l|r}
\hline
  & AgeGrp & Nh\\
\hline
15-24 & 15-24 & 634920\\
\hline
25-34 & 25-34 & 678513\\
\hline
35-45 & 35-45 & 659286\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

and for Gender

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Gender.benchmarks }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{kbl}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} \FunctionTok{kable\_styling}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{full\_width=}\ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|r}
\hline
Gender & Nh\\
\hline
male & 984091\\
\hline
female & 988628\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

With these we can rake the design:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{surf.nonresp.des.rake }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rake}\NormalTok{(surf.nonresp.des, }
                              \AttributeTok{sample=}\FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{AgeGrp, }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Gender), }
                              \AttributeTok{population=}\FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(AgeGrp.benchmarks, Gender.benchmarks))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

and then calculate our estimates

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Income, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{surf.nonresp.des.rake)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean     SE
## Income 579.58 22.798
\end{verbatim}

We can compare estimated population totals with the Population benchmarks:

\begin{table}
\centering

The numbers of schools by type are in \texttt{stype.benchmnarks}

Select a two stage cluster sample - and then make estimates of the mean school

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{111}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{api2sc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ apipopc }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} 
  \FunctionTok{select.2sc}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cfraction=}\FloatTok{0.30}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mmin=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{clustervar=}\StringTok{"dnum"}\NormalTok{) }
\NormalTok{api2sc.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{ids=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{dnum}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{snum, }\AttributeTok{fpc=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{bigN1}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{bigN2, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{api2sc) }
\FunctionTok{table}\NormalTok{(api2sc}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{stype)}\SpecialCharTok{/}\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(api2sc)}

\begin{Highlighting}[]
