\section{Replicate weights}\label{replicate-weights}

For some weight adjustments we no longer have a reliable method of computing
the standard errors of our estimates. This can occur when we have very
complex designs. It can also be necessary when we don't have access to the frame,
and therefore don't have all the information we need (such as population sizes in clusters)
to calculate standard errors.

In such circumstances we often create multiple sets of weights in addition to the
adjusted weights. These \textbf{replicate weights} are what are used to estimate the
standard errors, and they have the advantage that they can create standard errors
for a wider range of estimators than the usual means and totals.

The basic idea comes from recalling what standard errors are: they are the
variability we'd see in an estimate across all possible samples from the population
using the chosen sample design and sample size (Figure \ref{fig:sampling-from-population}).

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=17.78in]{include-files/fig/SamplingFromPopulation} 

}

\caption{Sampling error is the variation among the estimates that would be calculated from all possible samples from the population.}\label{fig:sampling-from-population}
\end{figure}

Once we've completed a survey we obviously can't take any more samples from the
population. Instead we use the variability we see within the \textbf{sample} to capture
and estimate the variability we'd see in different samples from the \textbf{population}
(Figrue \ref{fig:pseudo-sampling}).

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=17.78in]{include-files/fig/PseudoSampling} 

}

\caption{In Jacknife estimation sampling error can be estimated by calculating the variability among estimates from subsets taken from the sample.  This variability needs to be scaled up to get the true population variability, because the pseudo-samples resemble one another strongly, though not exactly.}\label{fig:pseudo-sampling}
\end{figure}

Instead of actually resampling from our sample, we instead just vary the weights
on some or all of the unit, and create new sets of weights according to a well defined
method. We do this multiple times, which is equivalent to taking multiple re-samples
of our actual sample. Each re-sample is called a \textbf{replicate} and has its own set of
weights for all of the units in the sample. e.g.~we might have 100 additional sets of
replicate weights attached to our survey data set, as well as the actual weights.

We then use these replicate weights to calculate a set of alternative
values for our estimate of interest. The variability among these replicate
estimates is then used to determine the standard error.

There are a number of different ways to create replicate weights: we'll consider just one
known as the \textbf{Jackknife}.

Each Jacknife replicate set of weights is calculated by deleting one or more PSUs at
the first stage of selection by setting their weights to zero. We then rescale the
weights of the remaining units so that they still add up to the same total weight (i.e so
they weights still add up to the population size). Table \ref{tab:examplejk} shows an example.

\begin{table}
\centering
\caption{\label{tab:examplejk}Example of Jacknife replicate weights: a sample of 4 clusters (PSUs) with sets of replicate weights, each one deleting the weight of a single PSU, and rescaling to maintain the total of the weights.}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|l|r|r|r|r|r}
\hline
id & psu & weight & rep01 & rep02 & rep03 & rep04\\
\hline
1 & A & 100 & 0.0 & 138.1 & 126.1 & 134.9\\
\hline
2 & A & 100 & 0.0 & 138.1 & 126.1 & 134.9\\
\hline
3 & A & 100 & 0.0 & 138.1 & 126.1 & 134.9\\
\hline
4 & B & 80 & 107.9 & 0.0 & 100.9 & 107.9\\
\hline
5 & B & 80 & 107.9 & 0.0 & 100.9 & 107.9\\
\hline
6 & B & 80 & 107.9 & 0.0 & 100.9 & 107.9\\
\hline
7 & B & 80 & 107.9 & 0.0 & 100.9 & 107.9\\
\hline
8 & C & 120 & 161.9 & 165.7 & 0.0 & 161.9\\
\hline
9 & C & 120 & 161.9 & 165.7 & 0.0 & 161.9\\
\hline
10 & D & 100 & 134.9 & 138.1 & 126.1 & 0.0\\
\hline
11 & D & 100 & 134.9 & 138.1 & 126.1 & 0.0\\
\hline
12 & D & 100 & 134.9 & 138.1 & 126.1 & 0.0\\
\hline
Total &  & 1160 & 1160.0 & 1160.0 & 1160.0 & 1160.0\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

We can create a set of replicate weights for the two stage cluster sample from the API
data set as follows:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{api2sc.jkdes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{as.svrepdesign}\NormalTok{(api2sc.des, }\AttributeTok{type=}\StringTok{"JK1"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## Warning in as.svrepdesign.default(api2sc.des, type = "JK1"): Finite population
## corrections after first stage have been dropped
\end{verbatim}

There is an informative warning here: no fpc can apply at second stage of selection
(nor any subsequent stages).

The specification \textbf{JK1} here indicates that the design is not stratified.

Here are the first 5 rows and 8 columns of replicate weights: each row refers to a PSU
(i.e.~a district \texttt{dnum}) and each column is a replicate.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{api2sc.jkdes}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{repweights}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{weights[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          [,1]     [,2]     [,3]     [,4]     [,5]     [,6]     [,7]     [,8]
## [1,] 0.000000 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641
## [2,] 1.025641 0.000000 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641
## [3,] 1.025641 1.025641 0.000000 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641
## [4,] 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641 0.000000 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641
## [5,] 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641 0.000000 1.025641 1.025641 1.025641
\end{verbatim}

Replicate Weights are \(n/(n-1)=40/39=1.025641\) - drops one of the 40 selected districts
\texttt{dnum} out for each replicate, setting the weight for all units selected in that district to zero.

These are called \textbf{scaling weights}, and they need to be multiplied by the
original sampling weight to get the actual replicate weights.

We can use this design just as in earlier examples to get estimates and standard errors:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{enroll, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{api2sc.jkdes)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean     SE
## enroll 711.45 82.848
\end{verbatim}

This gives us a good estimate of the SE, similar to what we get from the original
sample design specification:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{enroll, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{api2sc.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean     SE
## enroll 711.45 81.057
\end{verbatim}

Note that the estimate itself is identical: in both cases they come from the same single set
of sample weights.

If we are needing to post-stratify our weights to account for non-response, then
we need also to poststratify each set of replicate weights.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{api2sc.jkdes.ps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{postStratify}\NormalTok{(api2sc.jkdes, }\AttributeTok{strata=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, }\AttributeTok{population=}\NormalTok{stype.benchmarks)}
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{enroll, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{api2sc.jkdes.ps)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean    SE
## enroll 624.35 60.61
\end{verbatim}

Here post-stratification gives us a strong reduction of the standard errors, as we saw
earlier.

Here's a comparison of the various estimates and standard errors from the
various methods we've seen so far:

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|r|r}
\hline
  & Estimate & SE\\
\hline
Cenus & 619.0469 & 0.00000\\
\hline
2SC & 711.4530 & 81.05714\\
\hline
2SC+PS(stype) & 624.3485 & 53.14154\\
\hline
2SC+PS(stype x yr.rnd) & 629.4503 & 40.23927\\
\hline
2SC+Rake(stype+yr.rnd) & 632.0372 & 46.70502\\
\hline
JK1 & 711.4530 & 82.84780\\
\hline
JK1+PS(stype) & 624.3485 & 60.60973\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Important points to note:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  the SE is reduced by post-stratification, and
\item
  we get similar SEs when we use the jacknife estimation method.
\end{itemize}

Let's take a look at the replicate weights after post-stratification

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{api2sc.jkdes.ps}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{repweights}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{weights[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##      [,1]    [,2]     [,3]     [,4]     [,5] [,6]    [,7]     [,8]
## 1 0.00000 1.00628 1.047964 1.019081 1.000000 1.00 1.00628 1.012640
## 3 0.00000 1.00000 1.000000 1.000000 1.049550 1.00 1.00000 1.067176
## 1 1.00945 0.00000 1.047964 1.019081 1.000000 1.00 1.00628 1.012640
## 1 1.00945 1.00628 0.000000 1.019081 1.000000 1.00 1.00628 1.012640
## 2 1.00000 1.00000 0.000000 1.000000 1.030303 1.02 1.00000 1.000000
\end{verbatim}

They're no longer the same in the different PSUs.

\textbf{This seems to be a lot of additional work:} we get the same SEs and estimates
using the standard formula-based estimation methods for SEs. Why bother
with replicate weights in that case?

There are three reasons:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  For some very \textbf{complex designs} there are no formula based estimation methods
  that can be used;
\item
  For some \textbf{estimates}, such as medians and quartiles, replicate methods are
  more reliable than formula based methods;
\item
  Replicate based methods are robust in the presence of small samples and outliers,
  and can also be used to estimate bias (as well as standard errors).
\end{itemize}

It's commonly the case that rather than calculating them for ourselves we might
inestead have been \textbf{supplied} replicate weights, i.e.~someone else might have
created them. This often happens when we have been granted access
to a data set collected by a government department or agency concerned about
confidentiality, and in those instances we are not given access to the frame.

In which case the weights in the first 10 rows of our API two stage cluster dataset would look like this

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{r|r|r|r|r|r|r|r|r|r}
\hline
dnum & pweight & repweight001 & repweight002 & repweight003 & repweight004 & repweight005 & repweight006 & repweight007 & repweight008\\
\hline
31 & 41.16340 & 0.00000 & 41.42192 & 43.13775 & 41.94883 & 41.16340 & 41.16340 & 41.42192 & 41.68371\\
\hline
31 & 47.63519 & 0.00000 & 47.63519 & 47.63519 & 47.63519 & 49.99550 & 47.63519 & 47.63519 & 50.83511\\
\hline
44 & 27.44227 & 27.70160 & 0.00000 & 28.75850 & 27.96589 & 27.44227 & 27.44227 & 27.61462 & 27.78914\\
\hline
46 & 100.62165 & 101.57255 & 101.25359 & 0.00000 & 102.54158 & 100.62165 & 100.62165 & 101.25359 & 101.89352\\
\hline
46 & 53.99346 & 53.99346 & 53.99346 & 0.00000 & 53.99346 & 55.62963 & 55.07333 & 53.99346 & 53.99346\\
\hline
46 & 100.62165 & 101.57255 & 101.25359 & 0.00000 & 102.54158 & 100.62165 & 100.62165 & 101.25359 & 101.89352\\
\hline
51 & 41.16340 & 41.55241 & 41.42192 & 43.13775 & 0.00000 & 41.16340 & 41.16340 & 41.42192 & 41.68371\\
\hline
51 & 41.16340 & 41.55241 & 41.42192 & 43.13775 & 0.00000 & 41.16340 & 41.16340 & 41.42192 & 41.68371\\
\hline
67 & 47.63519 & 49.99550 & 47.63519 & 47.63519 & 47.63519 & 0.00000 & 47.63519 & 47.63519 & 50.83511\\
\hline
67 & 22.08824 & 22.08824 & 22.08824 & 23.79930 & 22.08824 & 0.00000 & 22.53000 & 22.08824 & 22.08824\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Unlike the example earlier, these replicate weights are \textbf{combined weights} -
i.e.~they do not need to be multiplied by the original weight, and they
each separately add up to the same value: the population size.

We use a data set like this to specify the sample design to R using just
the probability weights \texttt{pweight}and the set of replicate weights \texttt{repweight001-repweight040}.
We don't specify the cluster variables when specifying the design.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{pweightcol }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{match}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"pweight"}\NormalTok{,}\FunctionTok{names}\NormalTok{(api2sc.withrep.ps)) }\CommentTok{\# which column name is exactly equal to pweight}
\NormalTok{repweightcols }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{grep}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"\^{}repweight"}\NormalTok{,}\FunctionTok{names}\NormalTok{(api2sc.withrep.ps)) }\CommentTok{\# which columnnames start with repweight?}
\NormalTok{varcols }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\FunctionTok{ncol}\NormalTok{(api2sc.withrep.ps))}
\NormalTok{varcols }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ varcols[}\SpecialCharTok{!}\NormalTok{(varcols}\SpecialCharTok{\%in\%}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(pweightcol,repweightcols))]}
\NormalTok{n }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{unique}\NormalTok{(api2sc}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{dnum))}
\NormalTok{api2sc.repdes.ps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svrepdesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{variables=}\NormalTok{api2sc.withrep.ps[,varcols],}
                                \AttributeTok{weights=}\NormalTok{api2sc.withrep.ps[,pweightcol],}
                                \AttributeTok{repweights=}\NormalTok{api2sc.withrep.ps[,repweightcols],}
                                \AttributeTok{combined.weights=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{,}
                                \AttributeTok{scale=}\NormalTok{(n}\DecValTok{{-}1}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{n,}
                                \AttributeTok{type=}\StringTok{"JK1"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{enroll, api2sc.repdes.ps)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean     SE
## enroll 624.35 62.313
\end{verbatim}

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|r|r}
\hline
  & Estimate & SE\\
\hline
Cenus & 619.0469 & 0.00000\\
\hline
2SC & 711.4530 & 81.05714\\
\hline
2SC+PS(stype) & 624.3485 & 53.14154\\
\hline
2SC+PS(stype x yr.rnd) & 629.4503 & 40.23927\\
\hline
2SC+Rake(stype+yr.rnd) & 632.0372 & 46.70502\\
\hline
JK1 & 711.4530 & 82.84780\\
\hline
JK1+PS(stype) & 624.3485 & 60.60973\\
\hline
JK1+PS(stype); weights only & 624.3485 & 62.31259\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The consequence of only being given the weights (rather than the full clustering
information) means that the first stage design is treated as PPSWR rather than SRSWOR,
and there is no adjustment for the fpc. That means the SEs are slightly larger.

The method ``JK1'' is used for unstratified designs. For stratified
designs the jackknife is specified by ``JKn'': we don't just drop out one PSU over
the whole sample in each replicate, instead we drop out a PSU from every stratum
simultaneously.

E.g. draw a two stage cluster sample within two strata: schools which are or are
not eligible for an awards programme:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{999}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{apist2sc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ apipopc }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{select.st2sc}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n=}\DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{stratumvar=}\StringTok{"awards"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cfraction=}\FloatTok{0.20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{clustervar=}\StringTok{"dnum"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{apist2sc.des }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{svydesign}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{id=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{dnum}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{snum, }\AttributeTok{strata=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{awards, }\AttributeTok{fpc=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{bigN1}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{bigN2, }
                          \AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{apist2sc, }\AttributeTok{nest=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{apist2sc.des.ps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{postStratify}\NormalTok{(apist2sc.des, }\AttributeTok{strata=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, }\AttributeTok{population=}\NormalTok{stype.benchmarks)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Note: we need \texttt{nest=TRUE} here because districts \texttt{dnum} do not nest within strata.
I.e. it is the \textbf{combination} of \texttt{awards} and \texttt{dnum} that actually defines the clusters here.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{enroll, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apist2sc.des)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean     SE
## enroll 497.75 64.645
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{enroll, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apist2sc.des.ps)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean     SE
## enroll 523.97 63.001
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{apist2sc.jkdes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{as.svrepdesign}\NormalTok{(apist2sc.des, }\AttributeTok{type=}\StringTok{"JKn"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## Warning in as.svrepdesign.default(apist2sc.des, type = "JKn"): Finite
## population corrections after first stage have been dropped
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{enroll, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apist2sc.jkdes)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean     SE
## enroll 497.75 84.997
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{apist2sc.jkdes.ps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{postStratify}\NormalTok{(apist2sc.jkdes, }\AttributeTok{strata=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{stype, }\AttributeTok{population=}\NormalTok{stype.benchmarks)}
\FunctionTok{svymean}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{enroll, }\AttributeTok{design=}\NormalTok{apist2sc.jkdes.ps)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##          mean     SE
## enroll 523.97 82.125
\end{verbatim}

There are alternative methods to Jacknife generation of replicate weights.
You may hear of Bootstrap weights, and Balanced Repeated Replication. Some
of these have multiple variants, and there are settings in which one outperforms
the others. Jackknife variance estimation is the most common however.

\chapter{Imputation}\label{imputation}

In the previous chapter we saw methods of dealing with \textbf{unit non-response}: i.e.
we have units that are selected, but we collect no data at all from them.

Another common type of non-response is where a unit does not provide all the
information requested: certain data items are therefore missing. This is called
\textbf{item non-response}.

We could just do a complete case analysis - in other words throw all of the data away for
such units, and treat them as unit non-responders with zero weight. This wastes data and effort,
and misses the opportunity to use the data we have collected. It also leads to
a loss of precision due to reduced sample size.

An alternative option is \textbf{imputation}: this means that we \textbf{fill in (i.e.~invent)
the missing data} using some kind of model.

All such models involve untestable assumptions, and we run the same risks as when
we make weight adjustments for non-response. There is a substantial risk of bias
in our estimates whatever we do: leaving these units fully out of our analysis
risks missing an important part of the population. Incorrectly guessing their responses
is of course also a risk if we do so incorrectly. We may be \textbf{introducing} bias
in doing so.

Whatever we do our imputation must:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  be \textbf{minimal} - we should not impute more than a few per cent of our data;
\item
  \textbf{never be done on our key variables} - if we're trying to find out about political
  views then the last thing we should be doing is guessing them. Less significant
  variables, e.g.~household income, will have a lesser effect on our estimates;
\item
  be \textbf{documented} - we should always know how much imputation has been done,
  on which variables, and most importantly \textbf{how} the imputation was done.
\end{itemize}

Let's take as an example the SURF income survey synthetic dataset of 200 individuals
aged 15-45 from the NZ population. The top 10 rows are:

Let's remove some data: delete 10 entries at random from each of marital status and income.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{111}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{msurf }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ surf}
\NormalTok{msurf}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Marital[}\FunctionTok{sample}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(surf),}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{)] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ConstantTok{NA}
\NormalTok{msurf}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Income[}\FunctionTok{sample}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(surf),}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{)] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ConstantTok{NA}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Here are the counts of the various responses to the \texttt{Marital} variable after
removing 10 values.

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|r}
\hline
Marital & Freq\\
\hline
married & 68\\
\hline
never & 82\\
\hline
other & 20\\
\hline
previously & 20\\
\hline
NA & 10\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(mice)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(lattice)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The R package \texttt{mice} provides for multiple imputation methods, and provides some
demonstration datasets and some visualisation tools. However it's a bit tricky to use,
so we'll use a simpler function \texttt{impute()}.

The \texttt{mice} package can draw a nice picture of the pattern of missingness in the
SURF data, which is a mixture of numeric and categorical variables

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(msurf)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

An example dataset supplied with \texttt{mice} is \texttt{nhanes}, and is a tiny
extract of 25 rows from the US National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey
(NHANES). Note all variables are stored numeric, though the \texttt{age} and \texttt{hyp} column
are actually categorical.

We'll create a copy of the dataset, call it \texttt{mnhanes} and recode the factor levels.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{mnhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ mice}\SpecialCharTok{::}\NormalTok{nhanes}
\NormalTok{mnhanes}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{age }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{factor}\NormalTok{(mnhanes}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{age)}
\NormalTok{mnhanes}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{hyp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{factor}\NormalTok{(mnhanes}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{hyp)}
\FunctionTok{levels}\NormalTok{(mnhanes}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{age) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"20{-}39"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"40{-}59"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"60+"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{levels}\NormalTok{(mnhanes}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{hyp) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"No"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"Yes"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{mnhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{id=}\FunctionTok{factor}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(mnhanes)),mnhanes)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Here are the first 10 rows of the data, which include a lot of missing
data for the variables \texttt{bmi} (body mass index, kg/m\(^2\)), \texttt{hyp} (presence of hypertension, 1=no, 2=yes)
and \texttt{chl} (total serum cholesterol level, mg/dL).

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|l|r|l|r}
\hline
id & age & bmi & hyp & chl\\
\hline
1 & 20-39 & NA & NA & NA\\
\hline
2 & 40-59 & 22.7 & No & 187\\
\hline
3 & 20-39 & NA & No & 187\\
\hline
4 & 60+ & NA & NA & NA\\
\hline
5 & 20-39 & 20.4 & No & 113\\
\hline
6 & 60+ & NA & NA & 184\\
\hline
7 & 20-39 & 22.5 & No & 118\\
\hline
8 & 20-39 & 30.1 & No & 187\\
\hline
9 & 40-59 & 22.0 & No & 238\\
\hline
10 & 40-59 & NA & NA & NA\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Item non-response is indicated by an \texttt{NA} value. Notice that units 1, 4 and 10
provided no data at all apart from their ages.

Here's the missingness pattern in the NHANES dataset.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{md.pattern}\NormalTok{(mnhanes)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{_main_files/_main_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-264-1.pdf}}

\begin{verbatim}
##    id age hyp bmi chl   
## 13  1   1   1   1   1  0
## 3   1   1   1   1   0  1
## 1   1   1   1   0   1  1

\section{Mean, median or mode imputation}\label{mean-median-or-mode-imputation}

The simplest method of imputing a variable is just to replace any missing
value with a \textbf{typical} value from the units that did provide a response.

For numerical variables that can mean replacing missing values with the
mean or the median. (We prefer the median of course if there are outliers.)
For categorical variables the mode is the only \texttt{typical} value we have.

Let's replace the missing values of \texttt{bmi} in the \texttt{NHANES}mnhanes` dataset with the
mean of the responding values:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{mnhanes1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ mnhanes}
\NormalTok{mnhanes1}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{bmi.imp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(mnhanes1}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{bmi)}
\NormalTok{mnhanes1}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{bmi[}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(mnhanes1}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{bmi)] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(mnhanes1}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{bmi, }\AttributeTok{na.rm=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The code above does two things: in a new copy of the data set \texttt{mnhanes1}
it creates a new column \texttt{bmi.imp} which is \texttt{TRUE} if the \texttt{bmi} value is missing.\\
It then replaces \texttt{NA} values in the \texttt{bmi} column with the mean value
26.5625.

Here are the top 10 rows of the dataset after imputation.

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|l|r|l|r|l}
\hline
id & age & bmi & hyp & chl & bmi.imp\\
\hline
1 & 20-39 & 26.5625 & NA & NA & TRUE\\
\hline
2 & 40-59 & 22.7000 & No & 187 & FALSE\\
\hline
3 & 20-39 & 26.5625 & No & 187 & TRUE\\
\hline
4 & 60+ & 26.5625 & NA & NA & TRUE\\
\hline
5 & 20-39 & 20.4000 & No & 113 & FALSE\\
\hline
6 & 60+ & 26.5625 & NA & 184 & TRUE\\
\hline
7 & 20-39 & 22.5000 & No & 118 & FALSE\\
\hline
8 & 20-39 & 30.1000 & No & 187 & FALSE\\
\hline
9 & 40-59 & 22.0000 & No & 238 & FALSE\\
\hline
10 & 40-59 & 26.5625 & NA & NA & TRUE\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The function \texttt{impute()} does the same thing: we just specify the variable name
and the method (mean, median or mode).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{mhnanes1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{impute}\NormalTok{(mnhanes, }\AttributeTok{varname=}\StringTok{"bmi"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{method=}\StringTok{"mean"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

We can impute the categorical \texttt{hyp} variable using mode imputation
and the numerical \texttt{chl} variable using median imputation

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{mnhanes1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ mnhanes1 }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{impute}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{varname=}\StringTok{"hyp"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{method=}\StringTok{"mode"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{impute}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{varname=}\StringTok{"chl"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{method=}\StringTok{"median"}\NormalTok{) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This leads to the fully imputed dataset (first 10 rows only):

\begin{table}
\centering
\begin{tabular}[t]{l|l|r|l|r|l|l|l}
\hline
id & age & bmi & hyp & chl & bmi.imp & hyp.imp & chl.imp\\
\hline
1 & 20-39 & 26.5625 & No & 187 & TRUE & TRUE & TRUE\\
\hline
2 & 40-59 & 22.7000 & No & 187 & FALSE & FALSE & FALSE\\
\hline
3 & 20-39 & 26.5625 & No & 187 & TRUE & FALSE & FALSE\\
\hline
4 & 60+ & 26.5625 & No & 187 & TRUE & TRUE & TRUE\\
\hline
5 & 20-39 & 20.4000 & No & 113 & FALSE & FALSE & FALSE\\
\hline
6 & 60+ & 26.5625 & No & 184 & TRUE & TRUE & FALSE\\
\hline
7 & 20-39 & 22.5000 & No & 118 & FALSE & FALSE & FALSE\\
\hline
8 & 20-39 & 30.1000 & No & 187 & FALSE & FALSE & FALSE\\
\hline
9 & 40-59 & 22.0000 & No & 238 & FALSE & FALSE & FALSE\\
\hline
10 & 40-59 & 26.5625 & No & 187 & TRUE & TRUE & TRUE\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

There's an obvious risk here: by filling in the
most common or most typical value in the dataset we are \textbf{reducing variability} among
the respondents and making the sample dominated by these typical values.

For example in Figure \ref{fig:hypbarplot-mean} the proportion of people with hypertension
reduces from 4/17=24\% to 4/25=16\% because all of the missing individuals are assigned
to the typical reponse of `no hypertension'.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/hypbarplot-mean-1} 

}

\caption{Distribution of the Hypertension variable before and after mean imputation}\label{fig:hypbarplot-mean}
\end{figure}

Likewise the distribution of BMI after mean imputation is far from realistic
with a large artificial peak at the mean (Figure \ref{fig:bmidistribution-mean}).

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/bmidistribution-mean-1} 

}

\caption{BMI distributions before and after mean imputation}\label{fig:bmidistribution-mean}
\end{figure}

If we are only imputing a small proportion of values this shouldn't matter,
but we'd prefer to have an imputation method that didn't introduce such
artefacts into the data.

\section{Hot deck imputation}\label{hot-deck-imputation}

An attractive alternative to replacing an observation with the mean, median
or mode is the choose another respondent at random, and copy over their
data. These respondents are referred to as `donors', who `donate' a copy of their
data to the respondent with missing data.

The \texttt{impute()} function does this, by specifying the method ``sample'':

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{222}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{mnhanes2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ mnhanes }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}} 
  \FunctionTok{impute}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{varname=}\StringTok{"bmi"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{method=}\StringTok{"sample"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{impute}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{varname=}\StringTok{"hyp"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{method=}\StringTok{"sample"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{impute}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{varname=}\StringTok{"chl"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{method=}\StringTok{"sample"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The improved effect of doing so can be seen in Figures \ref{fig:hypbarplot-sample}
(where tbe before and after proportions of people with hypertension are 4/17=24\% and 5/20=20\%)
and \ref{fig:bmidistribution-sample}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/hypbarplot-sample-1} 

}

\caption{Distribution of the Hypertension variable before and after Hot Deck imputation}\label{fig:hypbarplot-sample}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/bmidistribution-sample-1} 

}

\caption{BMI distributions before and after Hot Deck imputation}\label{fig:bmidistribution-sample}
\end{figure}

The Hot Deck method gets its name from the `deck' (like a deck of cards) of respondents
with complete information from which we draw at random. `Hot' refers to the fact that
we are using respondents from the same survey as the donors. `Cold' deck imputation uses
data from another external data source as a source of donors.

\textbf{Important note} - you'll get a different set of imputed values every time
you do a hot deck imputation - so two analysts imputing data on the same data set
will end up with different imputed values and therefore (slightly) different final estimates.
If imputation is \textbf{minimal} then this shouldn't be a problem.

\section{Cell methods}\label{cell-methods}

We can do better still. We know that characteristics such as BMI, hypertension
and cholesterol correlate strongly with age, and we know the age of everyone.

It makes sense to do mean, median, mode \textbf{and} hot deck imputation within
\textbf{imputation cells}. These cells are groups of respondents who have the same
characteristics: specified by a (set of) categorical variables. We have to know
which imputation cell the respondent with missing data belongs to, so we must
have complete data for the variables defining the imputation cells.

Once we have imputation cells defined we can do mean, median, mode or hot deck imputation
within the cells.

In the \texttt{impute()} function we define the cells with the right hand side of a model formula
which should only include categorical variables. If it includes numerical variables
then it is unlikely that there will be anyone in the same cell as the respondent who needs a donor.
So with a variable like age we use age groups to define the cells, rather than year of age.

Let's do cell mean imputation for BMI, cell hot deck for hypertension, and cell median for cholesterol,
all with cells defined by age group.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{333}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{mnhanes3 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ mnhanes }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{impute}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{varname=}\StringTok{"bmi"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{method=}\StringTok{"mean"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{formula=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{age) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{impute}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{varname=}\StringTok{"hyp"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{method=}\StringTok{"sample"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{formula=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{age) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{impute}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{varname=}\StringTok{"chl"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{method=}\StringTok{"median"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{formula=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{age) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

We can compare BMI values by age group in our original and three imputed datasets

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/bmi-comparison-1} 

}

\caption{BMI distribution by age group after various imputation methods}\label{fig:bmi-comparison}
\end{figure}

\section{Regression methods}\label{regression-methods}

A further extension of imputation methods leads us to \textbf{regression imputation}:
using the complete data respondents we fit a regression model for our variable
of interest, and then use it to predict the missing values. This works for numerical
as well as categorical predictors, and includes cell mean imputation as a special case.
As with the cell methods we need the respondents who need their values imputed
to have complete data on the predictor variables.

Let's switch to imputing the 10 missing weekly incomes in the SURF dataset \texttt{msurf}.

We first note that income depends clearly both on Gender and weekly hours worked:

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/income-hours-1} 

}

\caption{Weekly Income and Weekly Hours worked by Gender.  The solid line shows the relationship for Males and the dashed line for Females}\label{fig:income-hours}
\end{figure}

Let's impute the missing values by mean imputation and
and regression imputation. Our regression model will be
\[ \label{eq:surf-imp-model}
   \text{Income}_i = \beta_0 + \beta_1 \text{(Hours$_i$)} 
                     + \beta_2 I(\text{Gender$_i$=Female}) 
                     + \varepsilon_i
                     \qquad \text{where $\varepsilon_i\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim} N(0,\sigma^2)$}
\]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{msurf1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ msurf }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{impute}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{varname=}\StringTok{"Income"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{method=}\StringTok{"mean"}\NormalTok{) }
\NormalTok{msurf2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ msurf }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{impute}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{varname=}\StringTok{"Income"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{method=}\StringTok{"lm.mean"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{formula=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Hours}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{Gender) }
\NormalTok{msurf3 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ msurf }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{impute}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{varname=}\StringTok{"Income"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{method=}\StringTok{"lm.sim"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{formula=}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{Hours}\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{Gender) }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The results of the three imputation methods are shown in Figure \ref{fig:income-hours-impute}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics{_main_files/figure-latex/income-hours-impute-1} 

}

\caption{Weekly Income and Weekly Hours worked by Gender.  The solid line shows the relationship for Males and the dashed line for Females.  The green points are cell mean imputations, the blue points are regression mean imputations and the red points are regression simulation imputations.}\label{fig:income-hours-impute}
\end{figure}

The cell mean imputations (shown in green on the plot) are clearly very poor -
everyone gets the same value, and in general these values are much to high since
our missing values correspond to workers with in general few weekly hours.

The first regression imputation has method \texttt{lm.mean} fits the model in equation \eqref{eq:surf-imp-model},
and then predicts directly onto the fitted regression line:
\[
   \text{ImputedIncome}_i = \widehat{\beta}_0 + \widehat{\beta}_1 \text{(Hours$_i$)} 
                     + \widehat{\beta}_2 I(\text{Gender$_i$=Female}) 
\]
using the fitted regression parameters \((\widehat{\beta}_0, \widehat{\beta}_1, \widehat{\beta}_2)\).
These imputed values are shown in blue, and are exactly where we'd expect a person of the
given Gender and Hours worked to be earning.

This is very good, but in the spirit of hot deck imputation we can do even better:
we know that observations should scatter around the the regression line, rather than lying
directly on it. So the second regression method draws from the error distribution
\[
   \widehat{\varepsilon}_i \sim N(0,\widehat{\sigma}^2)
\]
using the fitted standard deviation \((\widehat{\sigma})\). So the imputed value is
the expected value plus this additional error, which bumps the prediction off the regression
line:
\[
   \text{ImputedIncome}_i = \widehat{\beta}_0 + \widehat{\beta}_1 \text{(Hours$_i$)} 
                     + \widehat{\beta}_2 I(\text{Gender$_i$=Female}) 
                     + \widehat{\varepsilon}_i
\]
These imputed values are shown in red in Figure \ref{fig:income-hours-impute}, and they
look just like real data. To get these imputed values we change the method from
``lm.mean'' to ``lm.sim'' to change from the mean (expected) income, to a simulated value.

\section{Multiple imputation}\label{multiple-imputation}

Instead of imputing a \textbf{single} value for each missing observation, we impute
several possible imputed values - we end up with multiple completed data sets,
in something of the same spirit as the replicate weights we get with the Jackknife..
The variability among estimates from these separate completed data sets gives
us additional insight into the effect of our imputations, and the uncertainty
we have associated with having missing data.

The confidence we can have regarding the true scale of the uncertainty depends
on how strongly we believe in the model we've used to impute.

The \texttt{mice} package implements multiple imputation, that's its major function.\\
It's more detail then we need for this course, but you should be aware that
you'll see multiple imputation methods from time to time.

\chapter{Designing Surveys}\label{designing-surveys}

The design of surveys is a massive topic, and we can't cover much here.
The key principle is that everything that is done in the design of a survey
should be done to meet the Objectives (the research goals) of the survey. Nothing
else matters.

For our purposes calculation of the sample size required when designing
a survey is the most significant single decision to make since it determines
the standard errors for the survey estimates. In real life the decision
about sample size is often severely constrained by available resources.

\section{Another interpretation of the Deff}\label{another-interpretation-of-the-deff}

Assume that for sample size \(n\) the variance of an estimator
in a specific design has the approximate formula
\[
    \bfa{Var}{\widehat{T}_\text{design}} = \frac{c_\text{design}}{n}
\]
then using the definition of the Deff, the ratio of the variance with respect to
SRSWOR, then
\begin{eqnarray}
   \bfa{Deff}{\widehat{T}_{\text{design}}} 
       &=& \frac{\bfa{Var}{\widehat{T}_{\text{design}}}}{\bfa{Var}{\widehat{T}_{\text{SRSWOR}}}}\\
       &=& \frac{c_\text{design}/n}{c_\text{SRSWOR}/n}\\ 
       &=& \frac{c_\text{design}}{c_\text{SRSWOR}}
\end{eqnarray}
So if we want to find the sample sizes at which the variances
in the design of interest and SRSWOR are equal, then we set
\begin{eqnarray}
   V_\text{design} &=& V_\text{SRSWOR}\\
   \frac{c_\text{design}}{n_\text{design}} &=& \frac{c_\text{SRSWOR}}{n_\text{SRSWOR}}\\
   n_\text{design} &=& \frac{c_\text{design}}{c_\text{SRSWOR}}n_\text{SRSWOR}\\
                   &=& \bfa{Deff}{\widehat{T}_\text{design}} n_\text{SRSWOR}
\end{eqnarray}
This means that a rough way to compute the sample size required in a complex design
is to first calculate the sample size we'd need in SRSWOR, and then scale it by
the Deff for the complex design.

If the Deff is 2 (as it may be in a cluster design for example), then we need twice
the sample size an SRSWOR would need in order to achieve the same standard error.

\section{Computing the Required Sample Size}\label{computing-the-required-sample-size}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Identify your \textbf{design estimate} -- this is the estimate that will
  underlie a headline you could imagine being written about your survey.
\item
  Identify the desired MOE, \(m\), for this design estimate, noting whether
  you want this MOE to apply to the overall estimate, or for that estimate in
  each of, say, \(H\) subgroups/strata.
\item
  Compute the sample size required, \(n_1\), assuming that you're going to select a
  Simple Random Sample and ignoring the fpc.

  For estimation of a mean \(\bar{Y}\) of some characteristic \(y\):
  \[
   n_1 = \left(\frac{Z}{MOE}\right)^2 s^2
  \]
  where \(Z\) is appropriate for the desired level of confidence and \(s\) is the
  estimated population standard deviation of the characteristic \(y\).

  For estimation of a proportion \(P\):
  \[
   n_1 = \left(\frac{Z}{MOE}\right)^2 p(1-p)
  \]
  where \(p\) is the estimated proportion, or 0.5 if really unknown.
\item
  If the sample size is a substantial proportion of the population or
  subgroup size \(N\), then apply the fpc adjustment:
  \[
     n_2 = \frac{n_1}{1+\frac{n_1}{N}}
  \]
  This adjusts for the fact that in a finite population we don't need
  quite as large a sample to achieve the same standard error. However
  if the sampling fraction (\(n/N\): the proportion of the population that
  is selected) is small, then the fpc adjustment has very little effect.
\item
  If you have \(H\) subgroups and are using equal allocation then multiply by \(H\)
  \[
     n_3 = Hn_2
  \]
  or alternatively sum over the \(n_2\) values in each of the strata you've
  defined.
\item
  Apply the design effect (Deff) appropriate for the actual design you'll
  be using.
  \[
     n_4 = \text{Deff}\times n_3
  \]
\item
  Adjust for anticipated non-response: if response rate \(\phi\) is expected
  then
  \[
     n_5 = \frac{n_4}{\phi}
  \]
  (If non-response is likely to vary between strata, then apply this correction
  to \(n_2\), within each stratum.)
\end{enumerate}
